**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain why the water sent down the Frasch pump is at 170 °C and not at 100 °C.

1. Sulphur melts at about 115 °C.
2. Water at 100 °C is not hot enough to melt it.
3. Water is kept liquid above 100 °C by the pressure in the pipe, so it can be superheated to 170 °C.
4. At 170 °C the water is well above 115 °C and melts the sulphur underground.

**Answer:** because 115 °C is needed to melt sulphur, which is above the normal boiling point of water

Practice 5: `k4-1-steps` · ordering, level 3 · computed by rule

> Put the stages of the Frasch process in order.

Shown as: Superheated water at about 170 °C is forced down the outer pipe · The sulphur in the bed melts · The froth is pushed up the middle pipe and runs into tanks to solidify · Hot compressed air is forced down the inner pipe and froths the molten sulphur · Three concentric pipes are sunk down to the sulphur bed
Correct order: Three concentric pipes are sunk down to the sulphur bed → Superheated water at about 170 °C is forced down the outer pipe → The sulphur in the bed melts → Hot compressed air is forced down the inner pipe and froths the molten sulphur → The froth is pushed up the middle pipe and runs into tanks to solidify

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: Three concentric pipes are sunk down to the sulphur bed → Superheated water at about 170 °C is forced down the outer pipe → The sulphur in the bed melts → Hot compressed air is forced down the inner pipe and froths the molten sulphur → The froth is pushed up the middle pipe and runs into tanks to solidify.  

**Summary (the note)**

> Sulphur occurs free in underground beds, often near volcanoes and oil fields, and combined as sulphides (galena PbS, zinc blende ZnS, iron pyrites FeS2) and sulphates (gypsum CaSO4, barytes BaSO4).
> The Frasch process brings up free sulphur from deep beds using three concentric pipes:
> • Outer pipe: superheated water at about 170 °C, kept liquid under pressure, melts the sulphur (melting point about 115 °C).
> • Inner pipe: hot compressed air froths the molten sulphur.
> • Middle pipe: the froth, being less dense, is forced up to the surface.
> The rock and sand stay underground, so the sulphur comes up about 99 per cent pure. It is run into tanks and allowed to solidify.

## Lesson 1: Occurrence: Extraction of Sulphur by the Frasch process

Prerequisites: none · Skill: k4-1 (Describe the occurrence of sulphur and its extraction by the Frasch process)

**Note** (draft)

> Sulphur occurs free in underground beds, often near volcanoes and oil fields, and combined as sulphides (galena PbS, zinc blende ZnS, iron pyrites FeS2) and sulphates (gypsum CaSO4, barytes BaSO4).
> The Frasch process brings up free sulphur from deep beds using three concentric pipes:
> • Outer pipe: superheated water at about 170 °C, kept liquid under pressure, melts the sulphur (melting point about 115 °C).
> • Inner pipe: hot compressed air froths the molten sulphur.
> • Middle pipe: the froth, being less dense, is forced up to the surface.
> The rock and sand stay underground, so the sulphur comes up about 99 per cent pure. It is run into tanks and allowed to solidify.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to say where sulphur is found and describe each stage of the Frasch process and why it gives pure sulphur.
> Builds on: Form Three elements, mixtures and the idea of melting point.
> Leads to: Lesson 2 (the properties of sulphur) and the compounds made from it in the rest of the chapter.

**Learn: Where sulphur is found** (draft)

> Sulphur is found free (uncombined) in beds a few hundred metres underground, often near volcanoes and oil fields, notably in the United States, Mexico and Poland.
> It is also found combined:
> • as sulphides: galena PbS, zinc blende ZnS, iron pyrites FeS₂;
> • as sulphates: gypsum CaSO₄, barytes BaSO₄.
> Sulphur is also present in coal and crude oil, and in the proteins of living things.

**Learn: The Frasch process** (draft)

> Free sulphur deep underground is brought up by the Frasch pump, which has three concentric pipes.
> • Superheated water at about 170 °C is forced down the OUTER pipe. Pressure keeps it liquid above its normal boiling point, and it melts the sulphur, whose melting point is about 115 °C.
> • Hot compressed air is forced down the INNER pipe. It froths the molten sulphur.
> • The froth is less dense than water, so the pressure pushes it up the MIDDLE pipe to the surface, where it runs into tanks and solidifies.
> Because only the sulphur melts, the rock and sand stay underground and the sulphur comes up about 99 per cent pure.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **free element**: an element not joined to any other, as sulphur is in its beds
- **superheated water**: water kept liquid above 100 °C by pressure
- **Frasch process**: the method of mining underground sulphur with three concentric pipes
- **froth**: a light mixture of gas bubbles and liquid

- galena PbS: a sulphide
- gypsum CaSO₄: a sulphate
- water at 170 °C melts sulphur at 115 °C

Common mistake: “the compressed air burns the sulphur so it can rise” → “the air froths the molten sulphur so it is light enough to be pushed up”. Burning would destroy the sulphur. The air only makes a light froth.

## Lesson 2: Properties and allotropes of Sulphur

Prerequisites: 1 · Skill: k4-2 (Describe the physical and chemical properties of sulphur and its allotropes)

**Note** (draft)

> Sulphur is a brittle yellow solid, insoluble in water, soluble in carbon disulphide, and a poor conductor of heat and electricity.
> Allotropes are different forms of the same element in the same physical state. Sulphur has two crystalline allotropes:
> • Rhombic (alpha) sulphur: octahedral crystals, stable below about 96 °C, density 2.07 g/cm³.
> • Monoclinic (beta) sulphur: needle-shaped crystals, stable between about 96 °C and the melting point, density 1.98 g/cm³.
> 96 °C is the transition temperature. Plastic sulphur, a brown rubbery form made by pouring boiling sulphur into cold water, is not crystalline and slowly changes back to rhombic sulphur.
> Chemically, sulphur burns in air with a blue flame to sulphur dioxide, and combines with many metals on heating to form sulphides.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to describe the properties of sulphur and tell its allotropes apart by crystal shape, stability and density.
> Builds on: Lesson 1 (where sulphur comes from) and Form Three structure and bonding.
> Leads to: Lessons 3 to 8, which deal with the compounds sulphur forms.

**Learn: Physical properties and allotropes** (draft)

> Sulphur is a brittle yellow solid. It is insoluble in water, dissolves in carbon disulphide, does not conduct electricity, and melts at about 115 °C.
> Allotropes are different forms of the same element in the same physical state. Sulphur has two crystalline forms:
> • Rhombic (alpha): octahedral crystals, density 2.07 g/cm³, stable below about 96 °C. Grown by letting carbon disulphide evaporate from a solution of sulphur.
> • Monoclinic (beta): needle-shaped crystals, density 1.98 g/cm³, stable between about 96 °C and the melting point. Grown by letting molten sulphur cool slowly and pouring off the liquid left.
> About 96 °C is the transition temperature, where one form changes into the other.

**Learn: Plastic sulphur and chemical properties** (draft)

> If boiling sulphur is poured into cold water it sets as plastic sulphur: a brown rubbery solid with no crystals. It is unstable and slowly turns back into rhombic sulphur.
> Chemically, sulphur:
> • burns in air with a blue flame to sulphur dioxide: S + O₂ → SO₂;
> • combines with many metals on heating to give sulphides: Fe + S → FeS;
> • is oxidised by hot concentrated sulphuric acid and by hot concentrated nitric acid.
> It does not react with water or with dilute acids.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **allotropes**: different forms of the same element in the same physical state
- **rhombic sulphur**: the octahedral form, stable below about 96 °C
- **monoclinic sulphur**: the needle-shaped form, stable above about 96 °C
- **transition temperature**: the temperature at which one allotrope changes into the other

- rhombic: octahedral, 2.07 g/cm³
- monoclinic: needles, 1.98 g/cm³
- S + O₂ → SO₂

Common mistake: “rhombic and monoclinic sulphur are isotopes of sulphur” → “they are allotropes”. Isotopes differ in the nucleus. Allotropes differ in how the same atoms are arranged.

**Card c4k2-1** (draft)

> Allotropes are different forms of the same element in the same state.
>
> _rhombic and monoclinic sulphur are both pure solid sulphur_  

Check question: `k4-2-allo` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which allotrope of sulphur is stable below 96 °C?

- ◻️ Both are equally stable  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Only one form is stable at a given temperature._
- ◻️ Plastic sulphur  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Plastic sulphur is not stable at all: it slowly changes back to rhombic._
- ◻️ Monoclinic (beta) sulphur  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Monoclinic sulphur is the stable form between about 96 °C and the melting point._
- ✅ Rhombic (alpha) sulphur

Full working after a second miss:

> Below the transition temperature of about 96 °C, rhombic sulphur is the stable form.  
> So the answer is Rhombic (alpha) sulphur.  

**Card c4k2-2** (draft)

> Rhombic sulphur is stable below about 96 °C; monoclinic above it.
>
> _96 °C is the transition temperature_  

Check question: `k4-2-stable` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Which form of sulphur is stable at 100 °C?

- ◻️ Plastic sulphur  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Plastic sulphur is never the stable form: it slowly changes back to rhombic._
- ✅ Monoclinic (beta) sulphur
- ◻️ Rhombic (alpha) sulphur  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Rhombic is stable BELOW about 96 °C and monoclinic above it, up to the melting point._
- ◻️ Neither: sulphur is liquid at that temperature  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Sulphur melts at about 115 °C, so at 100 °C it is still solid._

Full working after a second miss:

> The transition temperature is about 96 °C.  
> 100 °C is above it, and the melting point is about 115 °C.  
> So the answer is: Monoclinic (beta) sulphur.

> Sulphur burns in air with a blue flame to give sulphur dioxide.
>
> _S + O₂ → SO₂_

- ◻️ A yellow flame, forming sulphur trioxide, SO₃

- ◻️ A white flame, forming a white solid

- ✅ A blue flame, forming sulphur dioxide, SO₂

Full working after a second miss:

> S + O₂ → SO₂, with a blue flame and a choking smell.  
> So the answer is A blue flame, forming sulphur dioxide, SO₂.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A sample of sulphur is kept at 76 °C for several days and then at 106 °C for several days. Name the stable form in each case.

1. The transition temperature is about 96 °C.
2. Below 96 °C the stable form is rhombic (alpha) sulphur, with octahedral crystals.
3. Between 96 °C and the melting point the stable form is monoclinic (beta) sulphur, with needle-shaped crystals.
4. 76 °C is below 96 °C, and 106 °C is above it.

**Answer:** rhombic at 76 °C and monoclinic at 106 °C

## Lesson 3: Laboratory preparation and physical properties of SO2

Prerequisites: 2 · Skill: k4-3 (Prepare sulphur dioxide in the laboratory and state its physical properties)

**Note** (draft)

> Laboratory preparation: a sulphite and a dilute acid.
> Na2SO3 + 2HCl → 2NaCl + H2O + SO2
> The acid is added to sodium sulphite in a flask; the gas is dried by passing it through concentrated sulphuric acid, and collected by downward delivery into a dry gas jar, because it is denser than air and very soluble in water.
> Sulphur dioxide can also be made by burning sulphur in air, or by heating copper with concentrated sulphuric acid.
> Physical properties: a colourless, poisonous gas with a sharp choking smell; denser than air (relative molecular mass 64); very soluble in water, giving an acidic solution; easily liquefied. It must be made in a fume cupboard.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to write the equation for preparing sulphur dioxide, explain how it is dried and collected, and state its physical properties.
> Builds on: Lesson 2 (sulphur burns to SO₂) and Form Three gas collection.
> Leads to: Lesson 4 (the chemical properties of SO₂) and the Contact process.

**Learn: Preparation** (draft)

> In the laboratory, sulphur dioxide is made from a sulphite and a dilute acid:
> Na₂SO₃ + 2HCl → 2NaCl + H₂O + SO₂
> The dilute acid is run onto sodium sulphite in a flask. The gas is led through a wash bottle of concentrated sulphuric acid, which dries it without reacting, and then down into a dry gas jar.
> It can also be made by burning sulphur in air, or by heating copper with concentrated sulphuric acid:
> Cu + 2H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + SO₂ + 2H₂O.

**Learn: Physical properties and collection** (draft)

> Sulphur dioxide is a colourless gas with a sharp choking smell. It is poisonous, so it must be prepared in a fume cupboard.
> Its relative molecular mass is 32 + 2 × 16 = 64, well above the average for air, so it is denser than air.
> It is very soluble in water, giving an acidic solution of sulphurous acid.
> Being dense and very soluble, it is collected by downward delivery into a dry gas jar: not over water, which would dissolve it.
> It is easily turned to a liquid by pressure, which is how it is stored and transported.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **sulphite**: a salt containing the SO₃ ion with a 2− charge, such as Na₂SO₃
- **downward delivery**: collecting a dense gas by leading it to the bottom of a jar
- **drying agent**: a substance that removes water from a gas without reacting with it
- **fume cupboard**: a cabinet that draws harmful fumes away

- Na₂SO₃ + 2HCl → 2NaCl + H₂O + SO₂
- RMM of SO₂ = 64

Common mistake: “sulphur dioxide is collected over water” → “it is collected by downward delivery”. SO₂ is very soluble in water, so collecting it over water would lose the gas.

**Card c4k3-1** (draft)

> A sulphite with a dilute acid gives sulphur dioxide.
>
> _Na₂SO₃ + 2HCl → 2NaCl + H₂O + SO₂_

## Lesson 4: Chemical properties of SO2

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: k4-4 (Describe the chemical properties of sulphur dioxide)

**Note** (draft)

> Sulphur dioxide is an acidic oxide, a reducing agent and a bleaching agent.
> • Acidic oxide: SO2 + H2O → H2SO3 (sulphurous acid), which turns blue litmus red. With alkalis it gives sulphites: SO2 + 2NaOH → Na2SO3 + H2O.
> • Reducing agent: it decolourises acidified potassium manganate(VII) (purple to colourless) and turns acidified potassium dichromate(VI) from orange to green. It also decolourises bromine water.
> • Bleaching agent: it bleaches by reduction, so the bleaching is temporary; the colour slowly returns as air oxidises the material. Chlorine bleaches by oxidation and is permanent.
> • As an oxidising agent with a stronger reducing agent: SO2 + 2H2S → 3S + 2H2O, giving a yellow deposit of sulphur.
> In the air, SO2 is oxidised to SO3 and dissolves in rain as sulphuric acid: this is acid rain.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to describe sulphur dioxide as an acidic oxide, a reducing agent, a bleach and sometimes an oxidising agent.
> Builds on: Lesson 3 (preparing SO₂) and Form Three acids, bases and redox.
> Leads to: Lesson 8 (sulphuric acid) and the problem of acid rain.

**Learn: An acidic oxide and a reducing agent** (draft)

> Sulphur dioxide is the oxide of a non-metal, so it is acidic.
> • With water: SO₂ + H₂O → H₂SO₃, sulphurous acid, which turns blue litmus red.
> • With an alkali: SO₂ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₃ + H₂O, giving a sulphite.
> It is also a good reducing agent, and the colour changes are used as tests:
> • acidified potassium manganate(VII): purple → colourless;
> • acidified potassium dichromate(VI): orange → green;
> • bromine water: brown → colourless.
> In each case the SO₂ is itself oxidised to a sulphate.

**Learn: Bleaching, oxidising and acid rain** (draft)

> Sulphur dioxide bleaches moist coloured materials such as flowers, straw, wool and paper. It does so by REDUCTION, so oxygen in the air slowly turns the colour back: the bleaching is temporary. Chlorine bleaches by oxidation, and that bleaching is permanent.
> With a stronger reducing agent, SO₂ is itself the oxidising agent:
> SO₂ + 2H₂S → 3S + 2H₂O, seen as a pale yellow deposit of sulphur.
> In the air, sulphur dioxide from burning coal and oil is oxidised and dissolves in rain as sulphuric acid. This acid rain damages buildings, kills fish in lakes and harms crops.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **acidic oxide**: an oxide that gives an acid with water and a salt with an alkali
- **reducing agent**: a substance that removes oxygen from, or gives electrons to, another
- **temporary bleach**: a bleach whose effect is slowly reversed by air
- **acid rain**: rain made acidic by dissolved oxides of sulphur and nitrogen

- SO₂ + H₂O → H₂SO₃
- purple → colourless with manganate(VII)
- SO₂ + 2H₂S → 3S + 2H₂O

Common mistake: “sulphur dioxide dissolves in water to give sulphuric acid” → “it gives sulphurous acid, H₂SO₃”. Sulphuric acid, H₂SO₄, comes from sulphur TRIoxide, SO₃.

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain, with an equation, what is seen when sulphur dioxide is bubbled through acidified potassium manganate(VII) solution.

1. Acidified potassium manganate(VII) solution is purple.
2. Sulphur dioxide is a reducing agent, so it reduces the manganate(VII) ion.
3. The purple colour disappears and the solution becomes colourless.
4. The SO₂ is itself oxidised to sulphate: it gains oxygen from the oxidising agent.

**Answer:** the purple colour disappears, because SO₂ reduces the manganate(VII)

## Lesson 5: Laboratory Preparation and physical properties of H2S

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: k4-5 (Prepare hydrogen sulphide in the laboratory and state its physical properties)

**Note** (draft)

> Laboratory preparation: a metal sulphide and a dilute acid.
> FeS + 2HCl → FeCl2 + H2S
> Iron(II) sulphide is placed in a flask and dilute hydrochloric acid is run onto it. No heat is needed. The gas is collected by downward delivery, because it is denser than air.
> Dilute sulphuric acid may be used instead. Nitric acid must not be used: it is an oxidising acid and gives sulphur instead.
> Physical properties: a colourless, very poisonous gas smelling of rotten eggs; denser than air (relative molecular mass 34); fairly soluble in water, giving a weakly acidic solution. It dulls the sense of smell, so it must always be made in a fume cupboard.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to write the equation for preparing hydrogen sulphide, explain how it is collected and state its properties and dangers.
> Builds on: Lesson 3 (preparing a gas and collecting it) and Form Three acids with salts.
> Leads to: Lesson 6 (the chemical properties of H₂S) and the sulphides of Lesson 7.

**Learn: Preparation** (draft)

> A metal sulphide and a dilute acid give hydrogen sulphide:
> FeS + 2HCl → FeCl₂ + H₂S
> Lumps of iron(II) sulphide are placed in a flask and dilute hydrochloric acid is run onto them from a thistle funnel. No heating is needed.
> Dilute sulphuric acid works as well. Nitric acid must NOT be used: it is an oxidising acid and would oxidise the hydrogen sulphide to sulphur, leaving a yellow deposit instead of a gas.

**Learn: Physical properties and safety** (draft)

> Hydrogen sulphide is a colourless gas with the smell of rotten eggs.
> Its relative molecular mass is 2 × 1 + 32 = 34, above the average for air, so it is denser than air and is collected by downward delivery.
> It is fairly soluble in water, giving a weakly acidic solution that turns blue litmus red.
> It is very poisonous. Worse, it quickly dulls the sense of smell, so a worker may stop noticing it while still breathing it in. It must always be prepared and used in a fume cupboard.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **sulphide**: a compound of a metal with sulphur, such as FeS
- **oxidising acid**: an acid such as nitric acid that oxidises what it reacts with
- **downward delivery**: collecting a dense gas at the bottom of a jar

- FeS + 2HCl → FeCl₂ + H₂S
- RMM of H₂S = 34

Common mistake: “hydrogen sulphide is prepared with dilute nitric acid” → “a non-oxidising acid such as hydrochloric acid is used”. Nitric acid would oxidise the gas to sulphur, so none would be collected.

**Card c4k5-1** (draft)

> A metal sulphide with a dilute acid gives hydrogen sulphide.
>
> _FeS + 2HCl → FeCl₂ + H₂S_

## Lesson 6: Chemical properties of H2S

Prerequisites: 5 · Skill: k4-6 (Describe the chemical properties of hydrogen sulphide)

**Note** (draft)

> Hydrogen sulphide is a weak acid, a reducing agent and it burns in air.
> • Weak acid: its solution turns blue litmus red. With alkalis it gives sulphides: H2S + 2NaOH → Na2S + 2H2O.
> • Burning: in plenty of air, 2H2S + 3O2 → 2SO2 + 2H2O (blue flame). In a limited supply, 2H2S + O2 → 2S + 2H2O, leaving a yellow deposit.
> • Reducing agent: it is oxidised to sulphur by many oxidising agents. With acidified potassium manganate(VII) the purple colour goes and sulphur appears; with chlorine water the colour goes; with sulphur dioxide: SO2 + 2H2S → 3S + 2H2O.
> • With metal salts it gives coloured sulphide precipitates, used in analysis: lead(II) nitrate gives black PbS. Lead(II) ethanoate paper turning black is the test for the gas.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to describe hydrogen sulphide as a weak acid, a reducing agent and a gas that burns, and give the test for it.
> Builds on: Lesson 5 (preparing H₂S) and Lesson 4 (redox reactions of SO₂).
> Leads to: Lesson 7 (the sulphides themselves) and the use of H₂S in analysis.

**Learn: Burning and acidity** (draft)

> Hydrogen sulphide burns in air with a blue flame, and what it gives depends on how much oxygen it has.
> • Plenty of air: 2H₂S + 3O₂ → 2SO₂ + 2H₂O.
> • Limited air: 2H₂S + O₂ → 2S + 2H₂O, leaving a yellow deposit.
> In water it is a weak acid: the solution turns blue litmus red, and with sodium hydroxide it gives a sulphide:
> H₂S + 2NaOH → Na₂S + 2H₂O.

**Learn: A reducing agent, and the test** (draft)

> Hydrogen sulphide gives up hydrogen easily, so it is a good reducing agent. It is itself oxidised to sulphur, which appears as a pale yellow deposit:
> • acidified potassium manganate(VII): purple → colourless, with sulphur;
> • chlorine water: the colour goes, with sulphur;
> • sulphur dioxide: SO₂ + 2H₂S → 3S + 2H₂O. Here SO₂ is the oxidising agent.
> With solutions of metal salts it gives sulphides of different colours, which is why it is used in analysis:
> Pb(NO₃)₂ + H₂S → PbS + 2HNO₃, a BLACK precipitate.
> The test for the gas is damp lead(II) ethanoate paper, which turns black.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **weak acid**: an acid only slightly split into ions in water
- **reducing agent**: a substance that gives electrons or hydrogen to another
- **precipitate**: an insoluble solid formed when two solutions are mixed
- **lead(II) ethanoate paper**: test paper that turns black with hydrogen sulphide

- 2H₂S + 3O₂ → 2SO₂ + 2H₂O
- PbS: black
- sulphur: a pale yellow deposit

Common mistake: “hydrogen sulphide burning in limited air gives sulphur dioxide” → “it gives sulphur”. With too little oxygen, only the hydrogen is oxidised, leaving the sulphur behind.

**Card c4k6-1** (draft)

> H₂S burns: plenty of air gives SO₂; limited air gives sulphur.
>
> _2H₂S + 3O₂ → 2SO₂ + 2H₂O_  

Check question: `k4-6-chem` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Hydrogen sulphide burns in a limited supply of air. What is seen?

- ◻️ A white solid forms  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No white solid is formed in this reaction._
- ◻️ The flame goes out at once  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It still burns; the limited air changes the product, not whether it burns._
- ◻️ A brown gas is given off  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A brown gas is nitrogen dioxide, which is not formed here._
- ✅ A yellow deposit of sulphur forms

Full working after a second miss:

> 2H₂S + O₂ → 2S + 2H₂O: with too little oxygen the sulphur is not oxidised.  
> So the answer is A yellow deposit of sulphur forms.  

**Card c4k6-2** (draft)

> H₂S is a reducing agent: it is oxidised to sulphur, seen as a yellow deposit.
>
> _SO₂ + 2H₂S → 3S + 2H₂O_

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain, with equations, what happens when hydrogen sulphide burns in plenty of air and in a limited supply of air.

1. In plenty of air there is enough oxygen to oxidise both the hydrogen and the sulphur.
2. 2H₂S + 3O₂ → 2SO₂ + 2H₂O, with a blue flame.
3. In a limited supply there is only enough oxygen for the hydrogen.
4. 2H₂S + O₂ → 2S + 2H₂O, leaving a yellow deposit of sulphur.

**Answer:** plenty of air gives SO₂ and water; limited air gives sulphur and water

## Lesson 7: Preparation of the Sulphides of Na and iron, and their Properties

Prerequisites: 6 · Skill: k4-7 (Prepare the sulphides of sodium and iron and describe their properties)

**Note** (draft)

> A sulphide is a compound of a metal with sulphur.
> Iron(II) sulphide: heat a mixture of iron filings and sulphur. Fe + S → FeS. The mixture glows and goes on reacting without more heating, because the reaction gives out heat. The product is a black solid.
> Sodium sulphide: made by heating sodium with sulphur, or by reducing sodium sulphate with carbon. It is a solid that dissolves easily in water to give an alkaline solution.
> A compound is not a mixture: a magnet picks iron out of a MIXTURE of iron and sulphur, but not out of FeS; the mixture shows yellow specks, while FeS is uniformly black.
> Both sulphides give hydrogen sulphide with a dilute acid: FeS + 2HCl → FeCl2 + H2S.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare the sulphides of iron and sodium, tell a compound from a mixture, and work out the masses that combine.
> Builds on: Lesson 6 (hydrogen sulphide) and Form One compounds and mixtures.
> Leads to: Lesson 8 (sulphuric acid) and the mole calculations of the third chapter.

**Learn: Making the sulphides** (draft)

> Iron(II) sulphide: mix iron filings with powdered sulphur and heat one spot strongly. The mixture begins to glow red and goes on reacting without further heating, because the reaction gives out heat:
> Fe + S → FeS
> The product is a black solid.
> Sodium sulphide: made by heating sodium with sulphur, or in industry by reducing sodium sulphate with carbon. It is a solid that dissolves readily in water, giving an alkaline solution.
> Both sulphides give hydrogen sulphide with a dilute acid, which is how the gas is prepared.

**Learn: Compound against mixture** (draft)

> A mixture of iron and sulphur and the compound FeS look different and behave differently:
> • a magnet picks the iron out of the MIXTURE, but takes nothing from the compound;
> • the mixture shows yellow specks among grey, while the compound is uniformly black;
> • dilute acid gives odourless hydrogen from the mixture, but hydrogen sulphide from the compound;
> • a mixture can hold any proportions, but FeS always has 56 parts of iron to 32 of sulphur by mass.
> In a compound the elements have joined chemically and lost their own properties.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **sulphide**: a compound of a metal with sulphur
- **mixture**: substances together but not chemically joined
- **exothermic**: giving out heat, as the iron and sulphur reaction does
- **fixed proportions**: the fixed ratio by mass in which elements combine in a compound

- Fe + S → FeS
- FeS + 2HCl → FeCl₂ + H₂S
- 56 g of Fe needs 32 g of S

Common mistake: “a magnet separates iron from iron(II) sulphide” → “a magnet separates iron from a MIXTURE of iron and sulphur only”. In the compound the iron is chemically joined and the solid is not magnetic.

**Card c4k7-1** (draft)

> Fe + S → FeS: heating starts the reaction, which then continues on its own.
>
> _the mixture glows red without more heating_

Full working after a second miss:

> So the answer is It is a solid that reacts steadily with dilute acid, so the flow of gas is easy to control.

1. Hold a magnet near it: a mixture gives up its iron filings to the magnet, but the compound does not.
2. Look at it closely: a mixture shows yellow specks of sulphur among grey iron, but the compound is uniformly black.
3. Add dilute hydrochloric acid: the mixture gives hydrogen (odourless), the compound gives hydrogen sulphide (rotten eggs).
4. In a compound the elements have lost their own properties.

**Answer:** a magnet no longer picks out iron, and the solid is uniformly black

## Lesson 8: Chemical properties of sulphuric acid

Prerequisites: 4 · Skill: k4-8 (Describe the chemical properties of sulphuric acid)

**Note** (draft)

> Dilute sulphuric acid behaves as a typical strong acid:
> • with a metal above hydrogen: Zn + H2SO4 → ZnSO4 + H2;
> • with a base or basic oxide: CuO + H2SO4 → CuSO4 + H2O;
> • with a carbonate: Na2CO3 + H2SO4 → Na2SO4 + H2O + CO2.
> Concentrated sulphuric acid has three further properties:
> • Dehydrating agent: it removes hydrogen and oxygen as water. Sugar turns to a black spongy mass of carbon; blue copper(II) sulphate crystals turn white.
> • Oxidising agent when hot: Cu + 2H2SO4 → CuSO4 + SO2 + 2H2O.
> • A less volatile acid: it displaces more volatile acids from their salts. NaCl + H2SO4 → NaHSO4 + HCl.
> Safety: always add the acid to water, never water to the acid, because a great deal of heat is given out.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to describe dilute sulphuric acid as a typical acid and concentrated sulphuric acid as a dehydrating, oxidising and less volatile acid.
> Builds on: Lesson 4 (SO₂ and the acid made from it) and Form Three acids, bases and salts.
> Leads to: Lesson 9 (the uses of sulphur and its compounds) and salt preparations.

**Learn: The dilute acid** (draft)

> Dilute sulphuric acid behaves as a typical strong acid. It turns blue litmus red and reacts in the usual three ways:
> • with a metal above hydrogen: Zn + H₂SO₄ → ZnSO₄ + H₂;
> • with a base or a basic oxide: CuO + H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + H₂O;
> • with a carbonate: Na₂CO₃ + H₂SO₄ → Na₂SO₄ + H₂O + CO₂.
> Its salts are sulphates. Lead and barium sulphates are insoluble, which is why barium chloride solution with dilute hydrochloric acid is the test for a sulphate.

**Learn: The concentrated acid** (draft)

> Concentrated sulphuric acid has three extra properties:
> • DEHYDRATING agent: it removes hydrogen and oxygen as water. Sugar becomes a black spongy mass of carbon; blue copper(II) sulphate crystals turn white; paper and wood char. It also dries gases that do not react with it.
> • OXIDISING agent when hot: Cu + 2H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + SO₂ + 2H₂O. Copper is below hydrogen, so it could not react with the dilute acid at all.
> • LESS VOLATILE acid: on heating it drives more volatile acids out of their salts: NaCl + H₂SO₄ → NaHSO₄ + HCl.
> Safety: always add the acid slowly to water with stirring, never water to the acid, because a great deal of heat is given out.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **dehydrating agent**: a substance that removes hydrogen and oxygen as water
- **oxidising agent**: a substance that gives oxygen to, or takes electrons from, another
- **volatile**: easily turned into a vapour
- **water of crystallisation**: water joined into a crystal, as in blue CuSO₄ crystals

- Zn + H₂SO₄ → ZnSO₄ + H₂
- sugar → black carbon
- Cu + 2H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + SO₂ + 2H₂O

Common mistake: “copper reacts with dilute sulphuric acid to give hydrogen” → “copper does not react with the dilute acid at all”. Copper is below hydrogen in the reactivity series. Only the hot CONCENTRATED acid attacks it, giving SO₂.

**Card c4k8-1** (draft)

> Dilute sulphuric acid is a typical acid: metal → hydrogen, base → salt and water, carbonate → carbon dioxide.
>
> _Zn + H₂SO₄ → ZnSO₄ + H₂_  

Check question: `k4-8-chem` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Why must concentrated sulphuric acid be added to water, and never water to the acid?

- ◻️ The acid would become too weak  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Diluting always lowers the concentration; that is not the danger._
- ✅ Mixing gives out a great deal of heat, and water poured onto the acid can boil and spit acid out
- ◻️ Water would react chemically with the acid to give hydrogen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No hydrogen is given off; the danger is heat._

> Explain what is seen when concentrated sulphuric acid is poured onto blue copper(II) sulphate crystals, and name the property shown.

1. Blue copper(II) sulphate crystals contain water of crystallisation.
2. Concentrated sulphuric acid takes up water very strongly.
3. It removes the water from the crystals, and the solid turns white.
4. Removing water from a substance is dehydration, so the acid is acting as a dehydrating agent.

**Answer:** the blue crystals turn white; the acid is acting as a dehydrating agent

## Lesson 9: Uses of sulphur and its compounds

Prerequisites: 8 · Skill: k4-9 (State the uses of sulphur and its compounds)

**Note** (draft)

> Sulphur itself is used to:
> • make sulphuric acid, by far its largest use (the Contact process);
> • vulcanise rubber, linking the chains so that tyres are harder, stronger and more elastic;
> • make matches, gunpowder and fireworks, because it catches fire easily;
> • make fungicides, dusted or sprayed on crops;
> • make drugs and ointments for skin diseases.
> Sulphur dioxide is used to bleach wool, silk, straw and paper, to preserve dried fruit and fruit juice, and to make sulphuric acid.
> Sulphuric acid is used in fertilisers (ammonium sulphate), detergents, paints, dyes, plastics, car batteries, pickling steel before plating, and in refining petroleum. Because so many industries depend on it, the amount a country uses is a measure of how industrial it is.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to list the main uses of sulphur, sulphur dioxide and sulphuric acid, and say which property each use depends on.
> Builds on: Lessons 2 to 8, which give the properties behind each use.
> Leads to: the nitrogen chapter, where fertilisers made with sulphuric acid appear again.

**Learn: Sulphur and sulphur dioxide** (draft)

> Sulphur itself is used to:
> • make sulphuric acid, by far its largest use;
> • vulcanise rubber: heating rubber with sulphur links its chains, so tyres are harder, stronger and more elastic;
> • make matches, gunpowder and fireworks, because it catches fire easily;
> • make fungicides, as dust or as a lime-sulphur spray on crops;
> • make ointments and drugs for skin diseases.
> Sulphur dioxide bleaches wool, silk, straw and paper, which chlorine would damage. It also preserves dried fruit and fruit juice, because it kills bacteria and moulds.

**Learn: Sulphuric acid** (draft)

> Sulphuric acid is one of the most important chemicals made. It is used:
> • to make fertilisers, especially ammonium sulphate from ammonia;
> • to make detergents, paints, dyes, plastics and fibres;
> • as the electrolyte in lead-acid car batteries;
> • to pickle steel, cleaning the surface before it is plated;
> • in refining petroleum.
> Because so many industries depend on it, the amount a country uses is often taken as a measure of how industrial it is. Care is still needed: the acid is corrosive, and sulphur dioxide escaping from factories causes acid rain.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **vulcanising**: heating rubber with sulphur to link its chains
- **fungicide**: a substance that kills fungi such as mildew
- **electrolyte**: a liquid that conducts electricity by its ions
- **pickling**: cleaning a metal surface with acid before plating

- sulphur → sulphuric acid: the largest use
- SO₂: preserving fruit juice
- H₂SO₄: car batteries

Common mistake: “sulphur dioxide is used in car batteries” → “dilute sulphuric acid is used in car batteries”. The battery needs an electrolyte, which is the acid, not the gas.

## Lesson 11: Occurrence and properties of nitrogen

Prerequisites: none · Skill: k4-11 (Describe the occurrence and properties of nitrogen)

**Note** (draft)

> Nitrogen makes up about 78 per cent of the air by volume. It is also combined in proteins, in nitrates in the soil, and in ammonia.
> Laboratory preparation from air: remove carbon dioxide with sodium hydroxide, dry the air, then pass it over heated copper, which takes the oxygen. What is left is nitrogen with a little argon, so it is slightly denser than pure nitrogen.
> Pure nitrogen is made by heating ammonium nitrite: NH4NO2 → N2 + 2H2O.
> In industry it comes from the fractional distillation of liquid air.
> Properties: a colourless, odourless gas, almost insoluble in water, slightly less dense than air, and very unreactive because of the strong triple bond N≡N. It does not burn and does not support burning. It combines with magnesium (3Mg + N2 → Mg3N2), with hydrogen in the Haber process, and with oxygen in a lightning flash.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to say where nitrogen is found, prepare it from air, and explain its properties from its triple bond.
> Builds on: Form One the composition of air, and Form Three bonding.
> Leads to: Lesson 12 (ammonia) and Lesson 16, the nitrogen cycle.

**Learn: Where nitrogen is found, and how it is obtained** (draft)

> Nitrogen makes up about 78 per cent of the air by volume. It is also combined in the proteins of living things, in nitrates in the soil, and in ammonia.
> From air in the laboratory: pass air through sodium hydroxide solution to take out carbon dioxide, then through concentrated sulphuric acid to dry it, then over heated copper, which takes the oxygen:
> 2Cu + O₂ → 2CuO
> What is left is nitrogen with a little argon, so it is slightly DENSER than pure nitrogen.
> Pure nitrogen: heat ammonium nitrite, NH₄NO₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O.
> In industry: fractional distillation of liquid air, where nitrogen boils off first.

**Learn: Properties** (draft)

> Nitrogen is a colourless, odourless gas, almost insoluble in water, and very slightly less dense than air (relative molecular mass 28 against about 29 for air).
> It is very unreactive. Its two atoms are joined by a TRIPLE bond, N≡N, which needs a great deal of energy to break. It does not burn and does not support burning.
> It does react in hard conditions:
> • with burning magnesium: 3Mg + N₂ → Mg₃N₂;
> • with hydrogen under pressure and a catalyst (the Haber process): N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃;
> • with oxygen in a lightning flash or a hot engine: N₂ + O₂ → 2NO.
> Being unreactive makes it useful: packets of food and oil tanks are filled with it to keep oxygen out.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **triple bond**: three shared pairs of electrons between two atoms
- **inert**: very unreactive
- **fractional distillation**: separating liquids by their different boiling points
- **nitride**: a compound of a metal with nitrogen, such as Mg₃N₂

- air: about 78 per cent nitrogen
- 2Cu + O₂ → 2CuO
- 3Mg + N₂ → Mg₃N₂

Common mistake: “nitrogen is unreactive because its atoms have full outer shells” → “it is unreactive because of the strong triple bond”. A nitrogen ATOM has 5 outer electrons. It is the N≡N bond in the molecule that is hard to break.

**Card c4k11-1** (draft)

> Air is about 78 per cent nitrogen by volume.
>
> _with about 21 per cent oxygen and 1 per cent other gases_  

Check question: `k4-11-which` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> How is nitrogen obtained in industry?

- ◻️ By heating nitrates  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Heating nitrates gives oxygen and oxides of nitrogen, not pure nitrogen._
- ◻️ From natural gas  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Natural gas is mainly methane; it is a source of hydrogen, not nitrogen._
- ✅ By the fractional distillation of liquid air
- ◻️ By passing air over heated copper  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the laboratory method and is far too slow for industry._

Full working after a second miss:

> Air is liquefied and then distilled; nitrogen boils off first at −196 °C, before oxygen at −183 °C.  
> So the answer is By the fractional distillation of liquid air.  

**Card c4k11-2** (draft)

> Nitrogen is unreactive because of its strong triple bond.
>
> _N≡N needs a great deal of energy to break_  

Check question: `k4-11-unreactive-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> Nitrogen is used to fill packets of crisps and to flush oil tanks. Which property does this use?

- ◻️ It is denser than air, so it smothers a fire  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Nitrogen neither burns nor lets anything else burn, so flushing a tank removes the danger._
- ◻️ It puts out fires by cooling them

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain why nitrogen obtained by removing oxygen from air is slightly denser than nitrogen made from ammonium nitrite.

1. Removing carbon dioxide, water and oxygen from air leaves nitrogen and the noble gases.
2. The noble gases, mainly argon, are not removed by heated copper.
3. Argon has a relative atomic mass of 40, well above the 28 of a nitrogen molecule.
4. So the mixture is a little denser than pure nitrogen.

**Answer:** because it still contains argon, which is denser than nitrogen

## Lesson 12: Laboratory preparation and properties of ammonia

Prerequisites: 11 · Skill: k4-12 (Prepare ammonia and describe its properties)

**Note** (draft)

> Laboratory preparation: heat an ammonium salt with an alkali.
> 2NH4Cl + Ca(OH)2 → CaCl2 + 2NH3 + 2H2O
> The gas is dried with quicklime (not concentrated sulphuric acid, which would react with it) and collected by upward delivery, because it is less dense than air and extremely soluble in water.
> Physical properties: a colourless gas with a sharp, pungent smell; less dense than air (relative molecular mass 17); extremely soluble in water, as the fountain experiment shows; easily liquefied.
> Chemical properties:
> • Alkaline: its solution turns damp red litmus blue, the only common alkaline gas.
> • With acids it gives ammonium salts: NH3 + HCl → NH4Cl, seen as dense white fumes.
> • A reducing agent: it reduces heated copper(II) oxide, 3CuO + 2NH3 → 3Cu + N2 + 3H2O.
> • It burns in oxygen, but not in air.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare ammonia, explain how it is dried and collected, and describe it as an alkaline gas and a reducing agent.
> Builds on: Lesson 11 (nitrogen) and Form Three acids, bases and salts.
> Leads to: Lesson 17 (fertilisers made from ammonia) and the Haber process.

**Learn: Preparation and collection** (draft)

> Heat an ammonium salt with an alkali:
> 2NH₄Cl + Ca(OH)₂ → CaCl₂ + 2NH₃ + 2H₂O
> The mixture is heated in a slanting tube, so that the water that condenses runs away from the hot glass.
> Drying: with QUICKLIME, CaO. Concentrated sulphuric acid cannot be used, because the acid would react with the alkaline gas; anhydrous calcium chloride also forms a compound with it.
> Collection: by upward delivery into an inverted jar, because ammonia is less dense than air (relative molecular mass 17) and far too soluble to collect over water.

**Learn: Properties** (draft)

> Ammonia is a colourless gas with a sharp, pungent smell. It is extremely soluble in water: in the fountain experiment, a few drops of water in a flask of the gas lower the pressure so much that water rushes in as a fountain.
> • ALKALINE: it is the only common alkaline gas, and turns damp red litmus blue.
> • With acids it gives ammonium salts: NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl, seen as dense white fumes. This is the test for the gas.
> • REDUCING agent: 3CuO + 2NH₃ → 3Cu + N₂ + 3H₂O. The black solid turns pink-brown.
> • It burns in pure oxygen but not in air.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **alkaline gas**: a gas whose solution is an alkali; ammonia is the only common one
- **quicklime**: calcium oxide, CaO, a basic drying agent
- **upward delivery**: collecting a gas less dense than air in an inverted jar
- **fountain experiment**: a demonstration of how soluble ammonia is

- 2NH₄Cl + Ca(OH)₂ → CaCl₂ + 2NH₃ + 2H₂O
- NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl: white fumes

Common mistake: “ammonia is dried with concentrated sulphuric acid” → “it is dried with quicklime”. The acid reacts with the alkaline gas to give ammonium sulphate, so the gas would be lost.

**Card c4k12-1** (draft)

> An ammonium salt heated with an alkali gives ammonia.
>
> _2NH₄Cl + Ca(OH)₂ → CaCl₂ + 2NH₃ + 2H₂O_  

Check question: `k4-12-prep` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What is seen in the fountain experiment with ammonia?

- ◻️ The gas bubbles out through the water  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The gas is taken INTO the water, not pushed out._
- ◻️ The flask cracks  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No crack is needed; the pressure difference drives the fountain._
- ✅ Water rushes up into the flask as a fountain, turning red if litmus is present
- ◻️ The water stays where it is  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The gas dissolves so fast that the pressure in the flask falls at once._

Full working after a second miss:

> Ammonia is extremely soluble, so a drop of water lowers the pressure in the flask and the rest is pushed in.  
> So the answer is Water rushes up into the flask as a fountain, turning red if litmus is present.  

**Card c4k12-2** (draft)

> Ammonia is the only common alkaline gas: it turns damp red litmus blue.
>
> _with HCl it gives dense white fumes of NH₄Cl_

  _↳ feedback if chosen: NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl, a white solid that appears as a smoke._

Full working after a second miss:

> NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl, a white solid that appears as a smoke.  
> So the answer is: A rod dipped in concentrated hydrochloric acid gives dense white fumes.  

**Card c4k12-3** (draft)

> Ammonia is a reducing agent: it reduces heated copper(II) oxide to copper.
>
> _3CuO + 2NH₃ → 3Cu + N₂ + 3H₂O_

- ◻️ Black to white

Full working after a second miss:

> The black copper(II) oxide becomes pink-brown copper as the oxygen is taken away.  
> So the answer is Black to pink-brown.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain why ammonia is dried with quicklime and not with concentrated sulphuric acid.

1. A drying agent must remove water without reacting with the gas.
2. Ammonia is an alkaline gas.
3. Concentrated sulphuric acid is an acid, so it would react with the ammonia to give ammonium sulphate.
4. Quicklime, CaO, is a basic oxide, so it dries the gas without reacting with it.

**Answer:** because sulphuric acid would react with the alkaline gas, while quicklime does not

## Lesson 13: Lab. preparation and properties of nitric acid

Prerequisites: 12 · Skill: k4-13 (Prepare nitric acid and describe its properties)

**Note** (draft)

> Laboratory preparation: heat potassium or sodium nitrate with concentrated sulphuric acid in an all-glass apparatus and distil off the nitric acid.
> KNO3 + H2SO4 → KHSO4 + HNO3
> The less volatile sulphuric acid drives out the more volatile nitric acid. Glass is used because the hot acid attacks cork, rubber and metals.
> Industrially it is made by oxidising ammonia (the Ostwald process).
> Properties:
> • As an acid: with bases, basic oxides and carbonates it gives nitrates. Na2CO3 + 2HNO3 → 2NaNO3 + H2O + CO2.
> • As an oxidising agent: with metals it gives a nitrate and an oxide of nitrogen, not hydrogen. Dilute acid with copper gives NO; concentrated acid gives NO2.
> • It decomposes in light: 4HNO3 → 4NO2 + 2H2O + O2, so pure acid slowly turns yellow and is kept in dark bottles.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare nitric acid, explain why glass is used, and describe it as both an acid and an oxidising agent.
> Builds on: Lesson 8 (sulphuric acid as a less volatile acid) and Lesson 12 (ammonia).
> Leads to: Lesson 14 (what heat does to nitrates) and Lesson 15 (the oxides of nitrogen).

**Learn: Preparation** (draft)

> Heat potassium or sodium nitrate with concentrated sulphuric acid and distil:
> KNO₃ + H₂SO₄ → KHSO₄ + HNO₃
> Sulphuric acid is less volatile, so on heating it drives the more volatile nitric acid out of its salt, and the nitric acid distils over.
> The whole apparatus must be glass, because the hot acid attacks cork, rubber and most metals. The receiver is kept cool so that the acid condenses.
> Pure nitric acid is colourless, but it slowly decomposes in the light:
> 4HNO₃ → 4NO₂ + 2H₂O + O₂, and the brown NO₂ dissolving in it turns it yellow. It is kept in dark bottles.
> In industry it is made by oxidising ammonia in the Ostwald process.

**Learn: An acid and an oxidising agent** (draft)

> As an ACID, dilute nitric acid behaves normally with bases, basic oxides and carbonates, giving nitrates:
> • NaOH + HNO₃ → NaNO₃ + H₂O;
> • Na₂CO₃ + 2HNO₃ → 2NaNO₃ + H₂O + CO₂.
> With METALS it behaves differently, because it is a strong oxidising agent. Instead of hydrogen it gives an oxide of nitrogen:
> • dilute acid with copper: colourless NO, which turns brown in the air;
> • concentrated acid with copper: brown NO₂.
> The one common exception is very dilute acid with magnesium, which does give hydrogen.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **volatile**: easily turned into a vapour
- **oxidising acid**: an acid that oxidises what it reacts with, giving no hydrogen with most metals
- **distil**: boil a liquid and condense the vapour to collect it
- **nitrate**: a salt of nitric acid, such as NaNO₃

- KNO₃ + H₂SO₄ → KHSO₄ + HNO₃
- dilute acid + Cu → NO
- concentrated acid + Cu → NO₂

Common mistake: “copper and dilute nitric acid give hydrogen” → “they give nitrogen oxide, NO”. Nitric acid is an oxidising acid, and copper is below hydrogen in the reactivity series.

**Card c4k13-1** (draft)

> A nitrate with concentrated sulphuric acid gives nitric acid, distilled in all-glass apparatus.
>
> _KNO₃ + H₂SO₄ → KHSO₄ + HNO₃_  

Check question: `k4-13-prep` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Dilute nitric acid is added to sodium carbonate. What happens?

- ◻️ Nitrogen dioxide is given off  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Nitrogen dioxide comes from the concentrated acid with a metal, not from a carbonate._
- ✅ Carbon dioxide is given off and sodium nitrate is formed
- ◻️ Hydrogen is given off  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Hydrogen comes from a metal with a non-oxidising acid._
- ◻️ Nothing happens  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Any acid reacts with a carbonate._

Full working after a second miss:

> Na₂CO₃ + 2HNO₃ → 2NaNO₃ + H₂O + CO₂: here it behaves as an ordinary acid.  
> So the answer is Carbon dioxide is given off and sodium nitrate is formed.  

**Card c4k13-2** (draft)

> Nitric acid is an oxidising acid: with metals it gives an oxide of nitrogen, not hydrogen.
>
> _dilute acid with copper gives NO; concentrated acid gives NO₂_  

Check question: `k4-13-metal-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> Why is no hydrogen given off when copper reacts with nitric acid?

- ✅ Copper is below hydrogen in the reactivity series, and the acid oxidises instead
- ◻️ Copper does not react with nitric acid at all  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An oxidising acid gives an oxide of nitrogen rather than hydrogen._
- ◻️ Nitric acid contains no hydrogen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An oxidising acid gives an oxide of nitrogen rather than hydrogen._
- ◻️ Copper is above hydrogen in the reactivity series  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An oxidising acid gives an oxide of nitrogen rather than hydrogen._

Full working after a second miss:

> An oxidising acid gives an oxide of nitrogen rather than hydrogen.  
> So the answer is: Copper is below hydrogen in the reactivity series, and the acid oxidises instead.  

**Card c4k13-3** (draft)

> With bases and carbonates it behaves as an ordinary acid, giving nitrates.
>
> _Na₂CO₃ + 2HNO₃ → 2NaNO₃ + H₂O + CO₂_  

Check question: `k4-13-salt-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> Dilute nitric acid is added to copper(II) oxide. What is formed?

- ◻️ Copper and water  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: CuO + 2HNO₃ → Cu(NO₃)₂ + H₂O: a base with an acid gives a salt and water._
- ◻️ Copper(II) nitrate and hydrogen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: CuO + 2HNO₃ → Cu(NO₃)₂ + H₂O: a base with an acid gives a salt and water._
- ◻️ No reaction  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: CuO + 2HNO₃ → Cu(NO₃)₂ + H₂O: a base with an acid gives a salt and water._
- ✅ Copper(II) nitrate and water, giving a blue solution

Full working after a second miss:

> CuO + 2HNO₃ → Cu(NO₃)₂ + H₂O: a base with an acid gives a salt and water.  
> So the answer is: Copper(II) nitrate and water, giving a blue solution.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain why copper gives no hydrogen with nitric acid, although zinc gives hydrogen with dilute hydrochloric acid.

1. Copper is below hydrogen in the reactivity series, so it cannot displace hydrogen from any acid.
2. Zinc is above hydrogen, so with hydrochloric acid it does displace it.
3. Nitric acid is also a strong oxidising agent.
4. It oxidises copper to copper(II) nitrate and is itself reduced to an oxide of nitrogen: NO with the dilute acid, NO₂ with the concentrated acid.

**Answer:** copper is below hydrogen, and nitric acid oxidises instead, giving an oxide of nitrogen

## Lesson 14: Effect of heat on nitrates

Prerequisites: 13 · Skill: k4-14 (Describe the effect of heat on nitrates)

**Note** (draft)

> All nitrates decompose on heating, but what is left depends on the metal.
> • Sodium and potassium nitrates (very reactive metals) give the nitrite and oxygen:
> 2NaNO3 → 2NaNO2 + O2.
> • Most other metal nitrates give the metal oxide, brown nitrogen dioxide and oxygen:
> 2Pb(NO3)2 → 2PbO + 4NO2 + O2. Lead(II) nitrate crackles as it decomposes.
> • Silver and mercury nitrates (very unreactive metals) give the metal itself, nitrogen dioxide and oxygen:
> 2AgNO3 → 2Ag + 2NO2 + O2.
> • Ammonium nitrate is not a metal nitrate: it gives dinitrogen oxide and water, NH4NO3 → N2O + 2H2O.
> The more reactive the metal, the more stable its nitrate is to heat.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to predict and write equations for what heat does to the nitrate of any metal, and link it to the reactivity series.
> Builds on: Lesson 13 (nitrates as the salts of nitric acid) and Form Three the reactivity series.
> Leads to: Lesson 15 (the oxides of nitrogen given off) and testing for nitrates.

**Learn: Three patterns** (draft)

> Every nitrate decomposes on heating, but the products depend on how reactive the metal is.
> • VERY REACTIVE metals (sodium, potassium): the nitrite and oxygen only.
> 2NaNO₃ → 2NaNO₂ + O₂
> • MOST metals (calcium, magnesium, zinc, iron, lead, copper): the oxide, brown nitrogen dioxide and oxygen.
> 2Pb(NO₃)₂ → 2PbO + 4NO₂ + O₂
> Lead(II) nitrate crackles loudly as it decomposes, and the yellow PbO left is orange while hot.
> • VERY UNREACTIVE metals (silver, mercury): the metal itself, nitrogen dioxide and oxygen.
> 2AgNO₃ → 2Ag + 2NO₂ + O₂

**Learn: Ammonium nitrate, and what you see** (draft)

> Ammonium nitrate is not a metal nitrate, and it behaves differently again:
> NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O
> This gentle heating is how dinitrogen oxide, 'laughing gas', is prepared. Strong heating of ammonium nitrate is dangerous, because it can explode.
> What to look for when a nitrate is heated:
> • a colourless gas that relights a glowing splint: oxygen;
> • a brown gas that turns damp blue litmus red: nitrogen dioxide;
> • the colour of the solid left: white for a nitrite, yellow or black for an oxide, shiny for a metal.
> The rule to remember: the more reactive the metal, the more stable its nitrate.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **decompose**: break down into simpler substances
- **nitrite**: a salt containing the NO₂ ion with a 1− charge, such as NaNO₂
- **thermal stability**: how well a compound stands up to heat
- **reactivity series**: metals in order of how readily they react

- 2NaNO₃ → 2NaNO₂ + O₂
- 2Pb(NO₃)₂ → 2PbO + 4NO₂ + O₂
- NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O

Common mistake: “heating sodium nitrate gives sodium oxide and nitrogen dioxide” → “it gives sodium nitrite and oxygen”. Sodium is very reactive, so its nitrate decomposes only as far as the nitrite.

**Card c4k14-1** (draft)

> Sodium and potassium nitrates give the nitrite and oxygen only.
>
> _2NaNO₃ → 2NaNO₂ + O₂_

3. Sodium nitrate melts and gives off a colourless gas that relights a glowing splint; the solid left is white.
4. 2NaNO₃ → 2NaNO₂ + O₂.

**Answer:** lead nitrate gives PbO, NO₂ and O₂; sodium nitrate gives NaNO₂ and O₂

## Lesson 15: Oxides of Nitrogen: (Nitrogen dioxide, Nitrogen oxide and Dinitrogen oxide) Preparation and properties

Prerequisites: 14 · Skill: k4-15 (Prepare and describe the oxides of nitrogen)

**Note** (draft)

> Three oxides of nitrogen are studied.
> • Dinitrogen oxide, N2O ('laughing gas'): made by gently heating ammonium nitrate, NH4NO3 → N2O + 2H2O. Colourless, faintly sweet, fairly soluble, neutral. It relights a glowing splint, like oxygen, because it decomposes on the hot splint. Used with oxygen as a mild anaesthetic.
> • Nitrogen oxide, NO: made by warming copper with dilute nitric acid. Colourless and neutral, almost insoluble in water, but it turns brown at once in air: 2NO + O2 → 2NO2. That is the test for it.
> • Nitrogen dioxide, NO2: made by heating lead(II) nitrate, or by copper with concentrated nitric acid. A brown, poisonous, acidic gas, denser than air, very soluble: 2NO2 + H2O → HNO3 + HNO2.
> Nitrogen oxides from car engines and lightning take part in acid rain.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare and recognise the three oxides of nitrogen and tell them apart by colour and by simple tests.
> Builds on: Lesson 14 (nitrates give oxides of nitrogen on heating) and Lesson 13 (nitric acid with copper).
> Leads to: Lesson 16 (the nitrogen cycle) and the part these oxides play in acid rain.

**Learn: Dinitrogen oxide and nitrogen oxide** (draft)

> DINITROGEN OXIDE, N₂O, 'laughing gas':
> • made by gently heating ammonium nitrate: NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O;
> • colourless, faintly sweet, fairly soluble, neutral;
> • it RELIGHTS a glowing splint, because it decomposes on the hot splint and gives oxygen. So this test alone cannot tell it from oxygen;
> • used with oxygen as a mild anaesthetic.
> NITROGEN OXIDE, NO:
> • made by warming copper with DILUTE nitric acid;
> • colourless, neutral, almost insoluble, so it is collected over water;
> • it turns brown at once in air: 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂. This is its test.

**Learn: Nitrogen dioxide** (draft)

> NITROGEN DIOXIDE, NO₂:
> • made by heating lead(II) nitrate, or by warming copper with CONCENTRATED nitric acid;
> • a BROWN, poisonous gas, denser than air and very soluble;
> • acidic: 2NO₂ + H₂O → HNO₃ + HNO₂, so it turns damp blue litmus red.
> Telling the three apart: only NO₂ is brown to start with; NO turns brown when air reaches it; N₂O stays colourless and relights a splint.
> Nitrogen oxides are made in car engines and by lightning, and they help to cause acid rain and photochemical smog.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **anaesthetic**: a substance that takes away the feeling of pain
- **acidic oxide**: an oxide that gives an acid with water
- **smog**: a haze formed when sunlight acts on pollutants in the air

- NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O
- 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂
- 2NO₂ + H₂O → HNO₃ + HNO₂

Common mistake: “a gas that relights a glowing splint must be oxygen” → “it could also be dinitrogen oxide”. N₂O decomposes on the hot splint and gives oxygen, so it relights it too.

**Card c4k15-1** (draft)

> N₂O comes from gently heating ammonium nitrate and relights a glowing splint.
>
> _NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O_  

Check question: `k4-15-which` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Nitrogen dioxide is dissolved in water. What is formed?

- ◻️ Ammonia solution  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Ammonia is alkaline and is not formed from NO₂ and water._
- ◻️ Nitrogen and oxygen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The gas dissolves and reacts; it does not split into its elements._
- ✅ A mixture of nitric acid and nitrous acid
- ◻️ A neutral solution  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The solution turns blue litmus red, so it is acidic._

Full working after a second miss:

> 2NO₂ + H₂O → HNO₃ + HNO₂: an acidic oxide.  
> So the answer is A mixture of nitric acid and nitrous acid.  

**Card c4k15-2** (draft)

> NO is colourless but turns brown at once in air.
>
> _2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂_

- ✅ Nitrogen oxide turns brown when it meets air; dinitrogen oxide does not

- ◻️ Neither can be told from the other  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂, and NO₂ is brown._
- ◻️ Both turn brown in air  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂, and NO₂ is brown._

Full working after a second miss:

> 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂, and NO₂ is brown.  
> So the answer is: Nitrogen oxide turns brown when it meets air; dinitrogen oxide does not.  

**Card c4k15-3** (draft)

> NO₂ is brown and acidic: with water it gives nitric and nitrous acids.
>
> _2NO₂ + H₂O → HNO₃ + HNO₂_

- NO₂ → **Brown and acidic**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> N₂O → Colourless; relights a glowing splint  
> N₂ → Makes up most of the air  
> NO₂ → Brown and acidic  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A colourless gas relights a glowing splint. Give two gases it could be, and a test that tells them apart.

1. Oxygen relights a glowing splint.
2. Dinitrogen oxide also relights one, because it decomposes on the hot splint to give oxygen.
3. To tell them apart, mix the gas with nitrogen oxide, NO.
4. With oxygen the mixture turns brown at once, because 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂; with N₂O it does not.

**Answer:** oxygen or dinitrogen oxide; mixing with NO turns brown only with oxygen

**Practice** `k4-15-vol` · number, level 2 · computed by rule

> What volume of dinitrogen oxide, in dm³ at s.t.p., is given off when 0.5 mol of ammonium nitrate is gently heated?

Answer: **11.2 dm³**
- Typed **22.4** (double) → “2 is the coefficient of the WATER, not of the gas collected.”
- Typed **22** (mass) → “The question asks for a volume in dm³, not a mass in grams.”

Full working after a second miss:

> NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O: 1 mol gives 1 mol of gas.  
> At s.t.p. that is 0.5 × 22.4 = 11.2 dm³.  

> What volume of dinitrogen oxide, in dm³ at s.t.p., is given off when 2 mol of ammonium nitrate is gently heated?

Answer: **44.8 dm³**
- Typed **89.6** (double) → “2 is the coefficient of the WATER, not of the gas collected.”
- Typed **88** (mass) → “The question asks for a volume in dm³, not a mass in grams.”

Full working after a second miss:

> NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O: 1 mol gives 1 mol of gas.  
> At s.t.p. that is 2 × 22.4 = 44.8 dm³.  

**Practice** `k4-15-mass` · number, level 3 · computed by rule

> What mass of ammonium nitrate, in grams, is needed to make 2 mol of dinitrogen oxide?

Answer: **160 g**
- Typed **88** (gas) → “The question asks for the mass of the SALT, not of the gas.”
- Typed **0.025** (div) → “Mass = moles × molar mass.”

Full working after a second miss:

> NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O: 1 mol of the salt gives 1 mol of gas.  
> The relative formula mass of NH₄NO₃ is 80.  
> Mass = 2 × 80 = 160 g.  

> What mass of ammonium nitrate, in grams, is needed to make 0.25 mol of dinitrogen oxide?

Answer: **20 g**
- Typed **11** (gas) → “The question asks for the mass of the SALT, not of the gas.”
- Typed **0.0031** (div) → “Mass = moles × molar mass.”

Full working after a second miss:

> NH₄NO₃ → N₂O + 2H₂O: 1 mol of the salt gives 1 mol of gas.  
> The relative formula mass of NH₄NO₃ is 80.  
> Mass = 0.25 × 80 = 20 g.  

## Lesson 16: Lesson Lesson 15. The nitrogen cycle

Prerequisites: 15 · Skill: k4-16 (Describe the nitrogen cycle)

**Note** (draft)

> The nitrogen cycle is how nitrogen passes between the air, the soil and living things.
> • Fixation: nitrogen from the air is turned into compounds plants can use. By nitrogen-fixing bacteria (Rhizobium) in the root nodules of legumes such as beans and groundnuts; by lightning, which makes nitrogen oxides that reach the soil in rain; and by industry, in the Haber process.
> • Absorption: plant roots take in nitrates and build them into proteins. Animals get their protein by eating plants.
> • Decay: bacteria of decay break down dead plants, animals and waste into ammonium compounds.
> • Nitrification: nitrifying bacteria change ammonium compounds into nitrites (Nitrosomonas) and then nitrates (Nitrobacter).
> • Denitrification: in waterlogged soil, denitrifying bacteria change nitrates back into nitrogen gas, which returns to the air.
> Farmers keep the soil rich by rotating crops with legumes and by adding fertilisers.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to name and explain each stage of the nitrogen cycle and say how farming practices affect it.
> Builds on: Lesson 11 (nitrogen is unreactive) and Form Three food chains.
> Leads to: Lesson 17 (fertilisers) and the study of soil fertility in Biology and Agriculture.

**Learn: Getting nitrogen into living things** (draft)

> Nitrogen gas is 78 per cent of the air, but it is so unreactive that plants cannot use it. It must first be FIXED into a compound:
> • nitrogen-fixing bacteria (Rhizobium) in the root nodules of legumes such as beans, groundnuts and soya;
> • lightning: the flash is hot enough to make N₂ and O₂ combine, and the oxides dissolve in rain;
> • industry: the Haber process makes ammonia, which is turned into fertiliser.
> Plant roots absorb NITRATES from the soil and build them into proteins. Animals get their protein by eating plants.

**Learn: Returning nitrogen to the soil and the air** (draft)

> • DECAY: when plants and animals die, and in their waste, bacteria of decay break the protein down into ammonium compounds.
> • NITRIFICATION: nitrifying bacteria change these into nitrites (Nitrosomonas) and then into nitrates (Nitrobacter), which plants can use again.
> • DENITRIFICATION: in waterlogged soil, where there is little air, denitrifying bacteria change nitrates back into nitrogen gas, and the soil loses nitrogen to the air.
> Farmers work with the cycle: they rotate crops with legumes, plough crop residues back in, drain waterlogged land, and add fertilisers to replace the nitrogen taken away in the harvest.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **nitrogen fixation**: changing nitrogen gas into a usable compound
- **legume**: a plant such as a bean or groundnut with nitrogen-fixing root nodules
- **nitrification**: the change of ammonium compounds into nitrates by bacteria
- **denitrification**: the change of nitrates back into nitrogen gas

- Rhizobium in bean roots: fixation
- decay → ammonium compounds
- waterlogged soil: denitrification

Common mistake: “plants take nitrogen straight from the air” → “they take nitrates from the soil”. Only certain bacteria can use nitrogen gas; plants need it already fixed.

**Card c4k16-1** (draft)

> Fixation turns nitrogen gas into compounds plants can use.
>
> _by bacteria in root nodules, by lightning, and in the Haber process_

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Keeping insects away is not the reason for this rotation._
- ◻️ They take nitrates out of the soil faster  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Legumes add nitrogen to the soil rather than stripping it._

Full working after a second miss:

> Legumes carry nitrogen-fixing bacteria, so the soil is richer in nitrogen for the next crop.  
> So the answer is The bacteria in their root nodules put fixed nitrogen back into the soil.  

**Card c4k16-2** (draft)

> _decay gives ammonium compounds; nitrification gives nitrates_

**Worked example** (draft)

> Explain why planting beans in a field helps the maize grown there the following year.

1. Beans are legumes, and their roots carry nodules holding nitrogen-fixing bacteria.
2. These bacteria change nitrogen from the air into compounds the plant can use.
3. When the bean plants die and decay, that fixed nitrogen is left in the soil as ammonium compounds.
4. Nitrifying bacteria change these into nitrates, which the maize roots can take in.

**Answer:** the bacteria in the bean root nodules leave fixed nitrogen in the soil

## Lesson 17: Uses of nitrogen compounds

Prerequisites: 16 · Skill: k4-17 (State the uses of nitrogen and its compounds)

**Note** (draft)

> Nitrogen gas: filling packets of food and flushing oil tanks, because it is unreactive; as liquid nitrogen at −196 °C for quick-freezing food and storing medical samples.
> Ammonia: made in the Haber process and used to make fertilisers (ammonium sulphate, ammonium nitrate, urea), nitric acid (the Ostwald process), cleaning liquids, and as a refrigerant.
> Nitric acid: making fertilisers, explosives (TNT, nitroglycerine), dyes, drugs and plastics.
> Fertilisers replace the nitrogen taken out of the soil by crops, so yields stay high. Too much, however, is washed into rivers, where it makes water plants grow too fast; when these die and decay the water loses its oxygen and fish die. This is eutrophication.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to give the uses of nitrogen, ammonia and nitric acid, and explain how too much fertiliser harms rivers.
> Builds on: Lessons 11 to 16, which give the properties behind each use.
> Leads to: the mole calculations of the next chapter, used to work out how much fertiliser a crop needs.

**Learn: Uses** (draft)

> NITROGEN GAS is used where an unreactive atmosphere is needed: filling packets of crisps and other food so the fat does not go rancid, and flushing out oil tanks so that they cannot catch fire. As LIQUID nitrogen at −196 °C it quick-freezes food and stores blood and other medical samples.
> AMMONIA, made in the Haber process, is the starting point for:
> • fertilisers: ammonium sulphate, ammonium nitrate and urea;
> • nitric acid, by the Ostwald process;
> • cleaning liquids, and refrigeration, because it takes in much heat as it evaporates.
> NITRIC ACID is used for fertilisers, explosives such as TNT, dyes, drugs and plastics.
> DINITROGEN OXIDE is used with oxygen as a mild anaesthetic.

**Learn: Fertilisers and the environment** (draft)

> Every harvest takes nitrogen off the land in the grain or the leaves. Fertilisers put it back, so yields stay high and the same field can be used year after year.
> A fertiliser is judged partly by its percentage of nitrogen by mass: in NH₄NO₃ the nitrogen is 28 out of 80, which is 35 per cent.
> But nitrates are soluble, so rain washes them into streams. There they make water plants grow very fast; when these die, the bacteria that decay them use up the oxygen and the fish suffocate. This is eutrophication.
> Careful farmers use only what the crop needs and leave strips of grass beside streams.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **fertiliser**: a substance added to soil to supply the elements plants need
- **Haber process**: making ammonia from nitrogen and hydrogen
- **eutrophication**: the loss of oxygen in water caused by too many nutrients
- **urea**: CO(NH₂)₂, a nitrogen fertiliser made from ammonia and carbon dioxide

- 2NH₃ + H₂SO₄ → (NH₄)₂SO₄
- NH₄NO₃: 35 per cent nitrogen
- liquid nitrogen at −196 °C

Common mistake: “more fertiliser always means a better harvest” → “too much is washed into rivers and causes eutrophication”. Beyond what the crop can take up, the extra nitrate runs off and kills fish.

**Card c4k17-1** (draft)

> Ammonia from the Haber process is the starting point for fertilisers and nitric acid.
>
> _2NH₃ + H₂SO₄ → (NH₄)₂SO₄_

  _↳ feedback if chosen: A fertiliser must not explode in the field; it is used for its nitrogen._
- ◻️ Making nitric acid

**Worked example** (draft)

1. 2NH₃ + H₂SO₄ → (NH₄)₂SO₄.
2. 2 mol of ammonia gives 1 mol of the fertiliser, so 2 mol gives 2 ÷ 2 = 1 mol.
3. The relative formula mass of (NH₄)₂SO₄ is 132.
4. Mass = 1 × 132.

**Answer:** 132 g

## Lesson 19: LesLesson 17. Definitions: relative atomic mass, relative molecular mass, molecular mass, molecular volume

Prerequisites: none · Skill: k4-19 (Define relative atomic mass, relative molecular mass, molar mass and molar volume)

**Note** (draft)

> Relative atomic mass (Ar): the average mass of the atoms of an element compared with one twelfth of the mass of a carbon-12 atom. It is a ratio, so it has no unit. Ar(H) = 1, Ar(C) = 12, Ar(O) = 16, Ar(Cl) = 35.5.
> Relative molecular mass (Mr): the sum of the relative atomic masses of all the atoms in a molecule. Mr(H2O) = 2 × 1 + 16 = 18.
> For an ionic compound, which has no molecules, the same sum is called the relative formula mass. Mr(NaCl) = 23 + 35.5 = 58.5.
> Molar mass: the mass of one mole of a substance, in g/mol. Its number is the same as the relative atomic or molecular mass.
> Molar volume: the volume of one mole of ANY gas, 22.4 dm³ at s.t.p. (0 °C and one atmosphere).

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to define and use relative atomic mass, relative molecular mass, molar mass and molar volume.
> Builds on: Form Three relative atomic mass and the mole of atoms.
> Leads to: Lesson 20 (the mole and the Avogadro constant) and every calculation in this chapter.

**Learn: Relative masses** (draft)

> Atoms are far too light to weigh one at a time, so their masses are compared with a standard.
> RELATIVE ATOMIC MASS (Ar) is the average mass of the atoms of an element compared with one twelfth of the mass of a carbon-12 atom. Being a ratio, it has no unit: Ar(H) = 1, Ar(C) = 12, Ar(O) = 16, Ar(Cl) = 35.5.
> RELATIVE MOLECULAR MASS (Mr) adds the relative atomic masses of all the atoms in a molecule:
> H₂O = 2 × 1 + 16 = 18; H₂SO₄ = 2 + 32 + 64 = 98.
> An ionic compound has no molecules, so the same sum is called the RELATIVE FORMULA MASS: NaCl = 23 + 35.5 = 58.5.

**Learn: Molar mass and molar volume** (draft)

> MOLAR MASS is the mass of one mole, in grams per mole. Its number is the same as the relative atomic or molecular mass, so one mole of water is 18 g and one mole of carbon dioxide is 44 g.
> MOLAR VOLUME is the volume of one mole of any GAS: 22.4 dm³ at s.t.p., which means 0 °C and one atmosphere.
> That the same volume holds a mole of any gas follows from Avogadro's law: equal volumes of gases at the same temperature and pressure hold equal numbers of molecules. In a gas the molecules are so far apart that their own size and mass hardly matter.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **relative atomic mass (Ar)**: the mass of an atom compared with one twelfth of a carbon-12 atom
- **relative molecular mass (Mr)**: the sum of the relative atomic masses in a molecule
- **molar mass**: the mass of one mole, in g/mol
- **molar volume**: 22.4 dm³/mol, the volume of one mole of any gas at s.t.p.
- **s.t.p.**: standard temperature and pressure: 0 °C and one atmosphere

- Mr(CO₂) = 44
- Mr(NaCl) = 58.5
- 1 mol of any gas = 22.4 dm³ at s.t.p.

Common mistake: “relative atomic mass is measured in grams” → “it has no unit at all”. It is a RATIO of two masses, so the units cancel. The molar mass, in g/mol, is the one with a unit.

**Card c4k19-1** (draft)

> Relative atomic mass compares an atom with one twelfth of a carbon-12 atom; it has no unit.
>
> _Ar(H) = 1, Ar(C) = 12, Ar(O) = 16, Ar(Cl) = 35.5_

- ◻️ The mass of one atom

- ✅ The mass of one mole of a substance, in grams per mole
- ◻️ The number of particles in one mole  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The number of particles is the Avogadro constant._

> So the answer is The mass of one mole of a substance, in grams per mole.  

**Card c4k19-2** (draft)

> Relative molecular mass is the sum of the relative atomic masses in the formula.
>
> _H₂O: 2 × 1 + 16 = 18_

Answer: **44 g**

> So 1 mole has a mass of 44 g.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Find the relative molecular mass of H₂SO₄ and state its molar mass.

1. Count the atoms: 7 in all.
2. Add the relative atomic masses: H is 1, S is 32 and O is 16.
3. 2 × 1 + 32 + 4 × 16 = 98.
4. The molar mass is the same number with the unit g/mol.

**Answer:** relative molecular mass 98; molar mass 98 g/mol

- ◻️ The volume of one mole of any liquid

- ◻️ The volume of one gram of a gas  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The molar volume is per MOLE, not per gram._

- ◻️ It has no mass  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It certainly has mass; the point is what the formula stands for._
- ◻️ Its molecules are too large  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Size is not the point; an ionic solid has no separate molecules at all._

Full working after a second miss:

> NaCl is the simplest ratio of ions in the lattice, so the mass found from it is a formula mass.  
> So the answer is Sodium chloride is ionic, so it has no molecules, only a lattice of ions.
