## Lesson 20: Definition of the mole, Avogadro’s number, L and its application to atoms, molecules and ions

Prerequisites: 19 · Skill: k4-20 (Define the mole and the Avogadro constant and apply them to atoms, molecules and ions)

**Note** (draft)

> A mole is the amount of a substance that holds as many particles as there are atoms in 12 g of carbon-12. That number is the Avogadro constant, L = 6.02 × 10²³ per mole.
> One mole of any substance holds 6.02 × 10²³ particles, whether they are atoms, molecules or ions.
> • 1 mol of helium = 6.02 × 10²³ atoms.
> • 1 mol of water = 6.02 × 10²³ molecules, but 3 × 6.02 × 10²³ atoms, because each molecule has 3 atoms.
> • 1 mol of NaCl gives 2 mol of ions: one of Na⁺ and one of Cl⁻.
> Number of particles = moles × L.
> The mass of one mole is the molar mass in g/mol, and one mole of any gas fills 22.4 dm³ at s.t.p.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to define the mole and the Avogadro constant, and count particles, atoms and ions in a given amount.
> Builds on: Lesson 19 (relative masses) and Form Three the mole of atoms.
> Leads to: Lesson 21, where moles, mass and volume are used together in calculations.

**Learn: The mole and the Avogadro constant** (draft)

> Chemists work with huge numbers of atoms, so they count them in moles.
> A MOLE is the amount of a substance that holds as many particles as there are atoms in 12 g of carbon-12. That number is the AVOGADRO CONSTANT, L = 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 per mole.
> One mole of any substance holds that many particles:
> • 1 mol of helium: 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 atoms;
> • 1 mol of carbon dioxide: 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 molecules;
> • 1 mol of sodium chloride: 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 formula units, which is 2 mol of ions.
> Number of particles = moles × L.

**Learn: Atoms, molecules and ions** (draft)

> Be careful to say WHICH particle is being counted.
> One mole of water, H₂O:
> • 1 mol of molecules;
> • 3 mol of atoms, because each molecule has 2 hydrogen and 1 oxygen;
> • 2 mol of hydrogen atoms and 1 mol of oxygen atoms.
> One mole of H₂SO₄ holds 7 mol of atoms: 2 of hydrogen, 1 of sulphur and 4 of oxygen.
> One mole of CaCl₂ gives 3 mol of ions: 1 of calcium and 2 of chloride.
> Together with the molar mass from Lesson 19, this links a mass you can weigh to a number of particles you cannot count.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **mole**: the amount of substance holding 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 particles
- **Avogadro constant (L)**: 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 per mole
- **formula unit**: the group of ions shown by the formula of an ionic compound
- **amount of substance**: the quantity measured in moles

- 1 mol of CO₂: 3 mol of atoms
- 2 mol of NaCl: 4 mol of ions
- particles = moles × L

Common mistake: “one mole of water holds 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 atoms” → “it holds that many MOLECULES, and three times as many atoms”. Each water molecule has 3 atoms, so a mole of molecules is 3 mol of atoms.

**Card c4k20-1** (draft)

> One mole holds 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 particles: the Avogadro constant.
>
> _1 mol of helium = 6.02 × 10 to the power 23 atoms_  

Check question: `k4-20-define` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ One third of the Avogadro number

- ✅ Three times the Avogadro number, because each molecule has 3 atoms
- ◻️ Twice the Avogadro number  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: There are 2 hydrogen atoms AND 1 oxygen atom: 3 in all._
- ◻️ The Avogadro number  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the number of MOLECULES, not of atoms._

Full working after a second miss:

> H₂O has 3 atoms per molecule, so a mole of molecules holds 3 moles of atoms.  
> So the answer is Three times the Avogadro number, because each molecule has 3 atoms.

>
> _2 mol of CO₂ = 1.2 × 10 to the power 24 molecules_

Answer: **0.3 mol**

**Worked example** (draft)

> How many molecules, and how many atoms, are there in 0.5 mol of H₂O?

1. Number of molecules = moles × L = 0.5 × 6.02 × 10 to the power 23.
2. = 3.01e+23 molecules.
3. Each molecule of H₂O holds 3 atoms.
4. So the number of atoms is 3 × 3.01e+23.

**Answer:** 3.01e+23 molecules and 9.03e+23 atoms

- Typed **0.0247** (div) → “Mass = moles × molar mass: multiply, do not divide.”
- Typed **81** (molar) → “81 g is the mass of ONE mole; here there are 2 mol.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Mass = moles × molar mass.  
> The molar mass of ZnO is 81 g/mol.  
> 2 × 81 = 162 g.

> 1 × 2 = 2 mol of O atoms.  

## Lesson 21: LLesson 19. Basic calculations on the mole concept

Prerequisites: 20 · Skill: k4-21 (Carry out basic calculations with moles, mass, particles and gas volume)

**Note** (draft)

> Three relations link a mole to things that can be measured.
> • moles = mass ÷ molar mass, so mass = moles × molar mass.
> • number of particles = moles × 6.02 × 10²³.
> • volume of a gas at s.t.p. = moles × 22.4 dm³, so moles = volume ÷ 22.4.
> Always write down what is given, change it to moles, and then change the moles to what is asked for.
> Example: 11 g of carbon dioxide. Molar mass 44 g/mol, so moles = 11 ÷ 44 = 0.25 mol; volume at s.t.p. = 0.25 × 22.4 = 5.6 dm³; molecules = 0.25 × 6.02 × 10²³.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to change between mass, moles, number of particles and gas volume in any direction.
> Builds on: Lesson 20 (the mole and the Avogadro constant) and Lesson 19 (molar mass and molar volume).
> Leads to: Lessons 22 to 27, where these steps are used inside larger calculations.

**Learn: The three links** (draft)

> Everything in this chapter uses three relations, with MOLES always in the middle.
> • mass: moles = mass ÷ molar mass, and mass = moles × molar mass;
> • particles: number = moles × L, where L = 6.02 × 10 to the power 23;
> • gas volume at s.t.p.: volume = moles × 22.4 dm³, and moles = volume ÷ 22.4.
> A question that gives one of these and asks for another is solved in two steps: change what is given into moles, then change the moles into what is asked for.

**Learn: Worked examples** (draft)

> 11 g of carbon dioxide:
> • molar mass 44 g/mol, so moles = 11 ÷ 44 = 0.25 mol;
> • volume at s.t.p. = 0.25 × 22.4 = 5.6 dm³;
> • molecules = 0.25 × 6.02 × 10 to the power 23.
> 5.6 dm³ of oxygen at s.t.p.:
> • moles = 5.6 ÷ 22.4 = 0.25 mol;
> • mass = 0.25 × 32 = 8 g.
> Always write the unit with the answer: mol, g or dm³. If the answer looks far too large or small, check whether you multiplied where you should have divided.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **molar mass**: the mass of one mole, in g/mol
- **s.t.p.**: standard temperature and pressure: 0 °C and one atmosphere
- **dm³**: a cubic decimetre, the same as a litre

- 9 g of H₂O = 0.5 mol
- 0.5 mol of gas = 11.2 dm³ at s.t.p.
- 2 mol = 1.204 × 10 to the power 24 particles

Common mistake: “moles = molar mass ÷ mass” → “moles = mass ÷ molar mass”. Check with a simple case: 44 g of CO₂ must be 1 mol, and 44 ÷ 44 = 1.

**Card c4k21-1** (draft)

> moles = mass ÷ molar mass.
>
> _44 g of CO₂ ÷ 44 g/mol = 1 mol_

Answer: **0.5 mol**

- Typed **2** (swap) → “moles = mass ÷ molar mass, not molar mass ÷ mass.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Molar mass of MgO = 40 g/mol.  
> moles = 20 ÷ 40 = 0.5 mol.  

**Card c4k21-2** (draft)

> mass = moles × molar mass.
>
> _0.5 mol of H₂O × 18 g/mol = 9 g_

Answer: **40 g**

Full working after a second miss:

> mass = 0.5 × 80 = 40 g.  

**Card c4k21-3** (draft)

>

## Lesson 22: Experimental determination of the formulae of magnesium oxide and copper oxide

Prerequisites: 21 · Skill: k4-22 (Find the formulae of magnesium oxide and copper oxide by experiment)

**Note** (draft)

> Magnesium oxide, by combination:
> Weigh a crucible and lid; add magnesium ribbon and weigh again. Heat strongly, lifting the lid from time to time to let air in without letting the white smoke escape. Heat, cool and weigh until the mass is constant.
> mass of Mg = (crucible + Mg) − crucible; mass of O = (crucible + product) − (crucible + Mg).
> Divide each mass by its relative atomic mass and take the simplest ratio: 1 : 1, so the formula is MgO.
> Copper oxide, by reduction:
> Weigh a dry tube holding copper(II) oxide. Pass dry hydrogen over it, letting the gas flow before lighting it, so that no air is left to explode. Heat; the black solid turns pink-brown. Cool in hydrogen and weigh.
> mass of O is the loss in mass. The ratio again gives CuO.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to describe both experiments and use the masses to work out the formula of the oxide.
> Builds on: Lesson 21 (moles from mass) and Form Three oxidation and reduction.
> Leads to: Lesson 24 (empirical formulae from any data) and practical work in the laboratory.

**Learn: Magnesium oxide, by combination** (draft)

> • Weigh a clean, dry crucible with its lid.
> • Add a coil of cleaned magnesium ribbon and weigh again.
> • Heat strongly, lifting the lid from time to time. Air must get in for the reaction, but the white smoke must not get out, or the mass of the product will be too small.
> • When all the magnesium has burned, cool and weigh. Heat, cool and weigh again until the mass is CONSTANT, which shows that the reaction is complete.
> mass of magnesium = (crucible + Mg) − crucible;
> mass of oxygen = (crucible + product) − (crucible + Mg).
> Divide each by its relative atomic mass: 2.4 ÷ 24 = 0.1 and 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1. The ratio is 1 : 1, so the formula is MgO.

**Learn: Copper oxide, by reduction** (draft)

> • Weigh a dry reduction tube holding copper(II) oxide.
> • Pass DRY hydrogen over it. Let the gas flow for a while BEFORE lighting it at the jet, so that all the air is swept out: a mixture of hydrogen and air explodes.
> • Heat the oxide. The black solid turns pink-brown as copper forms, and water collects in the cool part of the tube.
> • Cool in a stream of hydrogen, so that the hot copper cannot be oxidised again by air, then weigh.
> CuO + H₂ → Cu + H₂O
> mass of copper = final − tube; mass of oxygen = the loss in mass. 6.4 ÷ 64 = 0.1 and 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1: the ratio is 1 : 1, so the formula is CuO.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **constant mass**: a mass that no longer changes on further heating, showing the reaction is complete
- **crucible**: a small dish that stands strong heating
- **reduction**: removal of oxygen from a compound
- **simplest ratio**: the smallest whole-number ratio of the moles

- 2.4 g Mg + 1.6 g O → MgO
- 8 g CuO → 6.4 g Cu + 1.6 g O

Common mistake: “the mass of oxygen in the magnesium oxide experiment is the mass of the product” → “it is the product minus the magnesium”. The product holds both the magnesium and the oxygen; subtract to find the oxygen alone.

**Card c4k22-1** (draft)

> Heat, cool and weigh until the mass is constant: that shows the reaction is complete.
>
> _the mass rises as oxygen is taken in, then stops changing_  

Check question: `k4-22-method` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> In finding the formula of copper oxide, why is the hydrogen allowed to flow for a while before it is lit?

- ✅ To sweep all the air out of the tube, so the hydrogen does not explode
- ◻️ To dry the copper oxide  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The gas is dried before it reaches the tube, not by waiting._
- ◻️ To warm the copper oxide  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The flame does the heating, not the gas flow._
- ◻️ To measure the volume of hydrogen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No volume is measured in this experiment._

Full working after a second miss:

> A mixture of hydrogen and air explodes when lit, so the air must be swept out first.  
> So the answer is To sweep all the air out of the tube, so the hydrogen does not explode.  

**Card c4k22-2** (draft)

> Divide each mass by its relative atomic mass, then take the simplest ratio.
>
> _2.4 ÷ 24 = 0.1 and 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1: a ratio of 1 : 1_  

Check question: `k4-22-formula-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> 6 g of magnesium combines with 4 g of oxygen. What is the formula of the oxide?

- ◻️ Mg₂O  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That would need twice as many moles of magnesium as of oxygen; here they are equal._
- ✅ MgO
- ◻️ Mg₂O₃  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The moles come out equal, so the ratio is 1 : 1._
- ◻️ MgO₂  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That would need twice as many moles of oxygen as of magnesium; here they are equal._

Full working after a second miss:

> Moles of Mg = 6 ÷ 24 = 0.25; moles of O = 4 ÷ 16 = 0.25.  
> Divide both by the smaller to get the simplest ratio.  
> So the formula is MgO.  

**Card c4k22-3** (draft)

> Copper oxide is reduced by hydrogen: the mass falls as oxygen leaves.
>
> _CuO + H₂ → Cu + H₂O_  

Check question: `k4-22-copper-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> 40 g of copper(II) oxide is reduced by hydrogen and leaves 32 g of copper. What mass of oxygen, in grams, was in the oxide?

Answer: **8 g**
- Typed **72** (sum) → “The oxygen is the LOSS in mass: subtract.”
- Typed **32** (copper) → “32 g is the copper left behind.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The loss in mass is the oxygen removed.  
> 40 − 32 = 8 g.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> In an experiment, 2.4 g of magnesium gave 4 g of magnesium oxide. Find the formula.

1. Mass of oxygen = 4 − 2.4 = 1.6 g.
2. Moles of Mg = 2.4 ÷ 24 = 0.1.
3. Moles of O = 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1.
4. Divide both by the smaller: the ratio is 1 : 1.

**Answer:** MgO

- ◻️ To dry the copper oxide  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The gas is dried before it reaches the tube, not by waiting._
- ◻️ To warm the copper oxide  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The flame does the heating, not the gas flow._
- ✅ To sweep all the air out of the tube, so the hydrogen does not explode

Full working after a second miss:

> A mixture of hydrogen and air explodes when lit, so the air must be swept out first.  
> So the answer is To sweep all the air out of the tube, so the hydrogen does not explode.  

> Why is the crucible heated, cooled and weighed again, more than once?

- ◻️ To dry the crucible  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Drying is not the purpose, though the crucible must be dry at the start._
- ◻️ To make the crucible lighter  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The mass does not fall; it rises as oxygen is taken in._
- ◻️ To take the average of the readings  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The readings are not averaged; the aim is a mass that stops changing._
- ✅ To make sure the reaction is complete, shown by a constant mass

Full working after a second miss:

> Heating to CONSTANT MASS shows that all the magnesium has reacted.  
> So the answer is To make sure the reaction is complete, shown by a constant mass.

## Lesson 23: Calculations involving percentage composition by mass

Prerequisites: 21 · Skill: k4-23 (Calculate percentage composition by mass)

**Note** (draft)

> The percentage by mass of an element in a compound is:
> (mass of that element in one formula unit ÷ relative formula mass) × 100 per cent.
> Steps: work out the relative formula mass; multiply the relative atomic mass of the element by how many atoms of it there are; divide and multiply by 100.
> Example: water, H2O, Mr = 18. Hydrogen: 2 × 1 = 2, so 2/18 × 100 = 11.1 per cent. Oxygen: 16/18 × 100 = 88.9 per cent. The percentages add up to 100.
> The same idea tells a farmer which fertiliser carries the most nitrogen per bag, and tells a miner how much metal an ore holds.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to work out the percentage by mass of any element in a compound, and use it to compare fertilisers or ores.
> Builds on: Lesson 19 (relative formula mass) and Lesson 21 (mole calculations).
> Leads to: Lesson 24, which runs this calculation backwards to find a formula from percentages.

**Learn: The calculation** (draft)

> Percentage by mass of an element = (mass of that element in one formula unit ÷ relative formula mass) × 100.
> Water, H₂O, Mr = 18:
> • hydrogen: 2 × 1 = 2, so 2 ÷ 18 × 100 = 11.1 per cent;
> • oxygen: 16 ÷ 18 × 100 = 88.9 per cent.
> The two add up to 100, which is a useful check.
> Count EVERY atom of the element. In H₂SO₄ (Mr = 98) the oxygen is 4 × 16 = 64, so 64 ÷ 98 × 100 = 65.3 per cent.

**Learn: Why it matters** (draft)

> A farmer buying fertiliser wants the most nitrogen for the money, so the percentage of nitrogen matters more than the mass of the bag:
> • ammonium nitrate NH₄NO₃ (Mr 80): nitrogen 28 ÷ 80 × 100 = 35 per cent;
> • ammonium sulphate (NH₄)₂SO₄ (Mr 132): nitrogen 28 ÷ 132 × 100 = 21.2 per cent;
> • urea CO(NH₂)₂ (Mr 60): nitrogen 28 ÷ 60 × 100 = 46.7 per cent.
> In the same way, a miner works out how much metal an ore carries: haematite Fe₂O₃ is 70 per cent iron, so 100 tonnes of pure ore would give 70 tonnes of iron.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **percentage composition**: the percentage by mass of each element in a compound
- **relative formula mass**: the sum of the relative atomic masses in the formula
- **ore**: a rock from which a metal can be taken

- H₂O: 11.1 per cent H
- CaCO₃: 40 per cent Ca
- urea: 46.7 per cent N

Common mistake: “the percentage of oxygen in H₂SO₄ is 16 ÷ 98 × 100” → “it is 4 × 16 ÷ 98 × 100”. There are 4 oxygen atoms in the formula, so their total mass is 64.

**Card c4k23-1** (draft)

> Percentage = (mass of the element ÷ relative formula mass) × 100.
>
> _H₂O: hydrogen is 2 ÷ 18 × 100 = 11.1 per cent_  

Check question: `k4-23-pc` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Find the percentage by mass of S in H₂SO₄. Give your answer to 1 decimal place.

Answer: **32.7 %**
- Typed **306.3** (swap) → “Divide the mass of the ELEMENT by the whole formula mass.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Relative formula mass of H₂SO₄ = 98.  
> S: 1 × 32 = 32.  
> Percentage = 32 ÷ 98 × 100 = 32.7 per cent.  

**Card c4k23-2** (draft)

> Count every atom of the element in the formula.
>

## Lesson 24: Definition and determination of empirical and molecular formulae

Prerequisites: 23 · Skill: k4-24 (Find empirical and molecular formulae)

**Note** (draft)

> The empirical formula is the simplest whole-number ratio of the atoms in a compound. The molecular formula is the actual number of atoms in one molecule.
> To find an empirical formula from masses or percentages:
> 1. Write the mass (or percentage) of each element.
> 2. Divide each by its relative atomic mass: this gives the moles.
> 3. Divide every answer by the smallest of them.
> 4. If the numbers are not whole, multiply them all until they are.
> To go from the empirical formula to the molecular formula, divide the relative molecular mass by the mass of the empirical formula; the answer is how many times to multiply.
> Example: 85.7 per cent carbon and 14.3 per cent hydrogen gives CH2. If Mr = 42, then 42 ÷ 14 = 3, so the molecular formula is C3H6. Ionic compounds have only an empirical formula.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to find an empirical formula from masses or percentages, and turn it into a molecular formula using the relative molecular mass.
> Builds on: Lesson 23 (percentage composition) and Lesson 22 (formulae by experiment).
> Leads to: Lesson 25, where a balanced equation is used to find masses and volumes.

**Learn: Finding the empirical formula** (draft)

> The EMPIRICAL formula is the simplest whole-number ratio of the atoms. The MOLECULAR formula is the actual number in one molecule. Glucose is C₆H₁₂O₆, whose empirical formula is CH₂O.
> From masses or percentages:
> 1. Write the mass of each element (a percentage behaves like a mass in 100 g).
> 2. Divide each by its relative atomic mass: that gives the moles.
> 3. Divide every answer by the smallest.
> 4. If the answers are not whole numbers, multiply them all by 2 or 3 until they are.
> Example: 85.7 per cent C and 14.3 per cent H. 85.7 ÷ 12 = 7.14; 14.3 ÷ 1 = 14.3. Dividing by 7.14: 1 and 2. The empirical formula is CH₂.

**Learn: From empirical to molecular** (draft)

> The molecular formula is always a whole-number multiple of the empirical formula.
> Divide the relative molecular mass by the mass of the empirical formula:
> • CH₂ has a mass of 14. If Mr = 42, then 42 ÷ 14 = 3, so the molecular formula is C₃H₆.
> • If Mr = 56, then 56 ÷ 14 = 4, giving C₄H₈.
> Ionic compounds, such as NaCl and CaCO₃, have no molecules, so their formulae are always empirical: they show the ratio of ions in the lattice.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **empirical formula**: the simplest whole-number ratio of the atoms
- **molecular formula**: the actual number of atoms in one molecule
- **lattice**: the regular arrangement of ions in an ionic solid
- **simplest ratio**: the ratio after dividing by the smallest value

- C₆H₁₂O₆ → CH₂O
- CH₂ with Mr 42 → C₃H₆
- 2.4 g Mg + 1.6 g O → MgO

Common mistake: “the empirical formula of C₆H₁₂O₆ is C₆H₁₂O₆” → “it is CH₂O”. Divide every subscript by their highest common factor, which is 6.

**Card c4k24-1** (draft)

> Empirical formula: the simplest ratio. Molecular formula: the actual atoms in a molecule.
>
> _C₆H₁₂O₆ has the empirical formula CH₂O_  

Check question: `k4-24-define` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What is a molecular formula?

- ◻️ The formula of an ionic compound  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Ionic compounds have formula units, not molecules._
- ◻️ The simplest ratio of the atoms  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the EMPIRICAL formula._
- ◻️ The formula written with state symbols  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: State symbols are added to an equation; they are not part of the formula._
- ✅ The actual number of atoms of each element in one molecule

Full working after a second miss:

> Glucose has the molecular formula C₆H₁₂O₆, which is 6 times its empirical formula CH₂O.  
> So the answer is The actual number of atoms of each element in one molecule.  

**Card c4k24-2** (draft)

> Divide each mass or percentage by its relative atomic mass, then by the smallest answer.
>
> _2.4 ÷ 24 = 0.1; 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1; the ratio is 1 : 1_  

Check question: `k4-24-emp-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> A compound holds 4 g of Ca and 1.6 g of O. What is its empirical formula?

- ◻️ Ca₂O  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Work out the moles: 0.1 of Ca and 0.1 of O._
- ◻️ Ca₂O₃  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The simplest ratio of the moles gives CaO._
- ✅ CaO
- ◻️ CaO₂  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Divide each mass by its own relative atomic mass before comparing._

Full working after a second miss:

> Moles of Ca = 4 ÷ 40 = 0.1; moles of O = 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1.  
> Divide both by the smaller to get the simplest ratio.  
> So the empirical formula is CaO.  

**Card c4k24-3** (draft)

> Molecular formula = empirical formula × (Mr ÷ empirical formula mass).
>
> _CH₂ with Mr 42: 42 ÷ 14 = 3, so C₃H₆_  

Check question: `k4-24-mol-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> A compound has the empirical formula CH₂O and a relative molecular mass of 60. How many times does the empirical formula fit into the molecular formula?

Answer: **2**
- Typed **1800** (mult) → “Divide the relative molecular mass by the empirical formula mass.”
- Typed **0.5** (swap) → “Divide the LARGER mass by the smaller one.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The empirical formula mass of CH₂O is 30.  
> 60 ÷ 30 = 2.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A hydrocarbon is 85.7 per cent carbon and 14.3 per cent hydrogen, and its relative molecular mass is 42. Find its molecular formula.

1. Divide each percentage by the relative atomic mass: C 85.7 ÷ 12, H 14.3 ÷ 1.
2. That gives about 7.14 and 14.3 moles.
3. Divide both by the smaller: the ratio is about 1 : 2, so the empirical formula is CH₂.
4. The empirical formula mass is 14, and 42 ÷ 14 = 3.

**Answer:** C₃H₆

## Lesson 25: Calculation of masses and volume of gases from balanced chemical equations

Prerequisites: 24 · Skill: k4-25 (Calculate masses and volumes of gases from balanced equations)

**Note** (draft)

> A balanced equation gives the ratio of moles. That ratio is the key to every calculation.
> Steps:
> 1. Write the balanced equation.
> 2. Change what is given into moles (mass ÷ molar mass, or volume ÷ 22.4).
> 3. Use the ratio in the equation to find the moles of what is wanted.
> 4. Change those moles into a mass or a volume.
> Example: CaCO3 → CaO + CO2. 10 g of CaCO3 is 10 ÷ 100 = 0.1 mol, which gives 0.1 mol of CO2, that is 0.1 × 44 = 4.4 g, or 0.1 × 22.4 = 2.24 dm³ at s.t.p.
> For gases alone, the mole ratio is also the ratio of the volumes at the same temperature and pressure.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to use a balanced equation to find the mass or volume of any substance in a reaction.
> Builds on: Lesson 21 (mass, moles and volume) and Form Three balanced equations.
> Leads to: Lesson 26 (when one reactant runs out first) and every quantitative experiment.

**Learn: Moles are the bridge** (draft)

> A balanced equation gives the ratio of MOLES, not of masses. In
> CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂
> one mole of the carbonate gives one mole of each product, but their masses are 100 g, 56 g and 44 g.
> Every calculation has the same four steps:
> 1. write the balanced equation;
> 2. change what is given into moles;
> 3. use the ratio to get the moles of what is wanted;
> 4. change those moles into a mass or a volume.
> For 10 g of CaCO₃: moles = 10 ÷ 100 = 0.1; so 0.1 mol of CO₂; that is 0.1 × 44 = 4.4 g, or 0.1 × 22.4 = 2.24 dm³ at s.t.p.

**Learn: Volumes of gases** (draft)

> When every substance in the ratio is a gas, and all are measured at the same temperature and pressure, the mole ratio is also the VOLUME ratio. This follows from Avogadro's law.
> N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃
> So 1 dm³ of nitrogen needs 3 dm³ of hydrogen and gives 2 dm³ of ammonia; 10 dm³ of nitrogen gives 20 dm³ of ammonia, with no need to work out any moles at all.
> Be careful: this shortcut works only for gases. For a solid or a liquid in the equation, go through the moles.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **mole ratio**: the ratio of the coefficients in a balanced equation
- **excess**: more than enough, so that the other reactant decides the amount made
- **Avogadro's law**: equal volumes of gases at the same temperature and pressure hold equal numbers of molecules

- 10 g CaCO₃ → 4.4 g CO₂
- 1 dm³ N₂ → 2 dm³ NH₃

Common mistake: “100 g of CaCO₃ gives 100 g of CaO” → “it gives 56 g, because 44 g leaves as CO₂”. The equation balances MOLES, not masses; always change to moles first.

**Card c4k25-1** (draft)

> A balanced equation gives the ratio of MOLES, not of masses.
>
> _CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂ is 1 : 1 : 1 by moles_  

Check question: `k4-25-ratio` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> In the equation 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O, how many moles of water are made from 0.25 mol of oxygen?

Answer: **0.5 mol**
- Typed **0.25** (same) → “Read the coefficients: 1 mol of oxygen gives 2 mol of water.”
- Typed **0.125** (half) → “2 is the coefficient of the water, so multiply by 2.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The ratio of O₂ to H₂O is 1 : 2.  
> 0.25 × 2 = 0.5 mol.  

**Card c4k25-2** (draft)

> Change to moles, use the ratio, then change back.
>
> _10 g CaCO₃ → 0.1 mol → 0.1 mol CO₂ → 4.4 g_  

Check question: `k4-25-mass-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> What mass of calcium oxide, in grams, is made when 5 g of calcium carbonate decomposes completely?

Answer: **2.8 g**
- Typed **5** (same) → “Some mass leaves as carbon dioxide, so the oxide is lighter than the carbonate.”
- Typed **2.2** (co2) → “That is the mass of carbon dioxide given off.”

Full working after a second miss:

> CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂, a ratio of 1 : 1.  
> Moles of CaCO₃ = 5 ÷ 100 = 0.05 mol.  
> Mass of CaO = 0.05 × 56 = 2.8 g.  

**Card c4k25-3** (draft)

> For gases, the mole ratio is also the volume ratio.
>
> _N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃: 1 : 3 : 2 by volume_  

Check question: `k4-25-vol-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> What volume of carbon dioxide, in dm³ at s.t.p., is given off when 5 g of calcium carbonate decomposes completely?

Answer: **1.12 dm³**
- Typed **2.2** (mass) → “That is the MASS in grams; the question asks for a volume.”
- Typed **112** (nomole) → “Change the mass to moles first.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Moles of CaCO₃ = 5 ÷ 100 = 0.05 mol.  
> 1 mol gives 1 mol of CO₂.  
> Volume = 0.05 × 22.4 = 1.12 dm³.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> What mass of calcium oxide, and what volume of carbon dioxide at s.t.p., are made when 10 g of calcium carbonate decomposes completely?

1. CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂.
2. Moles of CaCO₃ = 10 ÷ 100 = 0.1 mol.
3. The ratio is 1 : 1 : 1, so the same number of moles of each product is made.
4. Mass of CaO = 0.1 × 56; volume of CO₂ = 0.1 × 22.4.

**Answer:** 5.6 g of CaO and 2.24 dm³ of CO₂

> CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂, a ratio of 1 : 1.  
> Moles of CaCO₃ = 100 ÷ 100 = 1 mol.  
> Mass of CaO = 1 × 56 = 56 g.

## Lesson 26: LLesson 24. Definition of Limiting reagents, theoretical and percentage yield, Molecular volumes

Prerequisites: 25 · Skill: k4-26 (Use limiting reagents, theoretical yield and percentage yield)

**Note** (draft)

> When two reactants are mixed, one is usually used up first. That one is the limiting reagent, and it decides how much product can be made. The other is said to be in excess.
> To find it: work out the moles of each reactant, divide each by its coefficient in the equation, and take the smallest.
> Theoretical yield: the mass of product the equation says should be made from the limiting reagent, if nothing is lost.
> Actual yield: the mass actually collected. It is almost always less, because product is lost in transferring, filtering and purifying, because reactions may not go to completion, and because side reactions use up reactant.
> Percentage yield = (actual yield ÷ theoretical yield) × 100 per cent. It can never be more than 100 per cent.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to find the limiting reagent, work out a theoretical yield, and calculate a percentage yield.
> Builds on: Lesson 25 (masses from equations) and Lesson 21 (mole calculations).
> Leads to: Lesson 27 (percentage yield in practice) and the costing of industrial processes.

**Learn: The limiting reagent** (draft)

> Mix 2 slices of bread with 10 slices of cheese and you can still make only 1 sandwich: the bread limits it. Chemistry works the same way.
> The LIMITING REAGENT is the reactant used up first; it decides how much product can be made. The other reactant is in EXCESS and is left over.
> To find it:
> 1. work out the moles of each reactant;
> 2. divide each by its coefficient in the balanced equation;
> 3. the smallest answer belongs to the limiting reagent.
> For N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃ with 0.3 mol of N₂ and 0.4 mol of H₂: 0.3 ÷ 1 = 0.3, and 0.4 ÷ 3 = 0.133. The hydrogen is limiting.

**Learn: Theoretical, actual and percentage yield** (draft)

> The THEORETICAL yield is what the equation says should be made from the limiting reagent, with nothing lost.
> The ACTUAL yield is what is really collected. It is almost always smaller, because:
> • some product sticks to the filter paper or the beaker;
> • some stays dissolved in the solvent;
> • the reaction may not go to completion;
> • side reactions use up some reactant.
> PERCENTAGE YIELD = (actual ÷ theoretical) × 100 per cent.
> If 10 g was expected and 8 g collected, the yield is 80 per cent. A yield above 100 per cent is impossible; if one is calculated, the product is probably still wet or impure.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **limiting reagent**: the reactant used up first, which decides the amount of product
- **excess**: more than enough of a reactant
- **theoretical yield**: the mass of product the equation predicts
- **percentage yield**: actual ÷ theoretical × 100

- 0.4 mol H₂ with 0.3 mol N₂: hydrogen limits
- 8 g of a possible 10 g: 80 per cent

Common mistake: “the reactant with the smaller mass is always limiting” → “divide the moles by the coefficient and compare”. A small mass of a light substance can still be many moles.

**Card c4k26-1** (draft)

> The limiting reagent runs out first and decides how much product is made.
>
> _the other reactant is in excess and is left over_  

Check question: `k4-26-define` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What is the theoretical yield?

- ✅ The mass of product that the equation says should be made, if nothing is lost
- ◻️ The mass of the reactants used  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The reactants are what you start with, not the yield._
- ◻️ The mass of product actually collected  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the ACTUAL yield._
- ◻️ The percentage of product that is pure  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Purity is a different idea._

Full working after a second miss:

> It is worked out from the limiting reagent and the mole ratio in the equation.  
> So the answer is The mass of product that the equation says should be made, if nothing is lost.  

**Card c4k26-2** (draft)

> Divide the moles of each reactant by its coefficient; the smallest is limiting.
>
> _0.3 ÷ 1 = 0.3 against 0.4 ÷ 3 = 0.133: the second is limiting_  

Check question: `k4-26-find-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> 0.1 mol of N₂ is mixed with 1.2 mol of H₂: N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃. Which is the limiting reagent?

- ✅ Nitrogen
- ◻️ Hydrogen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Compare 0.1 with 0.4: the SMALLER one runs out first._
- ◻️ Neither: they are exactly matched  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: 0.1 and 0.4 are not equal, so one runs out before the other._
- ◻️ The one with the larger number of moles  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Having more moles does not help if the equation needs 3 times as many._

Full working after a second miss:

> Divide each by its coefficient: nitrogen 0.1 ÷ 1 = 0.1; hydrogen 1.2 ÷ 3 = 0.4.  
> The smaller value belongs to the limiting reagent.  
> So the answer is: Nitrogen.  

**Card c4k26-3** (draft)

> Percentage yield = actual ÷ theoretical × 100.
>
> _8 g made out of a possible 10 g is 80 per cent_  

Check question: `k4-26-yield-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> A reaction should give 20 g of product, but only 16 g is collected. What is the percentage yield?

Answer: **80 %**
- Typed **125** (swap) → “Divide the ACTUAL yield by the theoretical one.”
- Typed **4** (diff) → “That is the mass lost, not the percentage yield.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Percentage yield = actual ÷ theoretical × 100.  
> 16 ÷ 20 × 100 = 80 per cent.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> 0.3 mol of nitrogen is mixed with 0.4 mol of hydrogen: N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃. Which is the limiting reagent, and how many moles of ammonia can be made?

1. Divide each by its coefficient: nitrogen 0.3 ÷ 1 = 0.3; hydrogen 0.4 ÷ 3 = 0.133.
2. The smaller is the hydrogen, so hydrogen is the limiting reagent.
3. The ratio of H₂ to NH₃ is 3 : 2.
4. Moles of ammonia = 0.4 × 2 ÷ 3 = 0.267 mol.

**Answer:** hydrogen is limiting; 0.267 mol of ammonia

**Practice** `k4-26-define` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ◻️ The reactant with the largest molar mass

- ◻️ The reactant there is most of  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The one in excess is left over; it is not what limits the reaction._
- ✅ The reactant that is used up first and so decides how much product is made
- ◻️ The product made in the smallest amount  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A limiting reagent is a reactant, not a product._

Full working after a second miss:

> Once the limiting reagent is gone the reaction stops, however much of the other reactant is left.  
> So the answer is The reactant that is used up first and so decides how much product is made.

## Lesson 27: Calculation of percentage yields of reactions

Prerequisites: 26 · Skill: k4-27 (Calculate the percentage yield of a reaction)

**Note** (draft)

> Percentage yield = (actual yield ÷ theoretical yield) × 100 per cent.
> To work one out:
> 1. Find the moles of the limiting reagent.
> 2. Use the equation to find the moles of product expected.
> 3. Change that to a mass: this is the theoretical yield.
> 4. Divide the actual yield by it and multiply by 100.
> A yield above 100 per cent is impossible; if one is calculated, the product is probably still wet or impure.
> Yields matter in industry: a low yield wastes raw material, so the cost of the reactants, the energy used and the purity of the product are all weighed up together when a process is chosen.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to calculate a percentage yield from experimental masses and explain why yields fall short.
> Builds on: Lesson 26 (theoretical yield and limiting reagents) and Lesson 25 (masses from equations).
> Leads to: Lesson 28 (balancing by the mole concept) and the costing of industrial processes.

**Learn: Working out a percentage yield** (draft)

> Percentage yield = (actual ÷ theoretical) × 100 per cent.
> The four steps:
> 1. find the moles of the limiting reagent;
> 2. use the equation to find the moles of product expected;
> 3. change that to a mass: the theoretical yield;
> 4. divide the actual yield by it and multiply by 100.
> Example: 10 g of CaCO₃ is heated and 4.4 g of CaO is collected.
> Moles of carbonate = 10 ÷ 100 = 0.1; so 0.1 mol of CaO, which is 0.1 × 56 = 5.6 g.
> Percentage yield = 4.4 ÷ 5.6 × 100 = 78.6 per cent.

**Learn: Why yields fall short, and why it matters** (draft)

> Common reasons:
> • product left on the filter paper, the beaker or the stirring rod;
> • product that stays dissolved in the solvent;
> • a reaction that does not go to completion, or that reverses;
> • side reactions making something else.
> A yield above 100 per cent is impossible, because atoms are conserved. If one is calculated, the product was probably weighed before it was dry, so water was counted as product. The cure is to dry to constant mass.
> In industry a low yield means buying more raw material for the same output, so it costs more. A process is chosen by weighing up yield, the cost of the reactants, the energy needed, the time taken and the purity of the product.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **actual yield**: the mass of product really collected
- **theoretical yield**: the mass the equation predicts
- **percentage yield**: actual ÷ theoretical × 100
- **constant mass**: the mass once no more water can be driven off

- 4.4 g of a possible 5.6 g: 78.6 per cent
- 8 g of a possible 10 g: 80 per cent

Common mistake: “a yield of 110 per cent shows an unusually good reaction” → “it shows the product was wet or impure”. Atoms are conserved, so no reaction can beat its theoretical yield.

**Card c4k27-1** (draft)

> Percentage yield = actual ÷ theoretical × 100.
>
> _8 g collected out of a possible 10 g is 80 per cent_  

Check question: `k4-27-why` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> A pupil calculates a percentage yield of 110 per cent. What is the most likely reason?

- ◻️ The reaction was very efficient  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No reaction can beat its theoretical yield._
- ◻️ The balance was set to zero  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Zeroing the balance is correct practice and would not raise the mass._
- ◻️ The theoretical yield was too large  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A theoretical yield that was too large would give a yield BELOW 100 per cent._
- ✅ The product was weighed before it was dry, so the water counted as product

Full working after a second miss:

> Water or unreacted material left in the product adds to the mass weighed.  
> So the answer is The product was weighed before it was dry, so the water counted as product.  

**Card c4k27-2** (draft)

> Work out the theoretical yield from the equation first.
>
> _10 g CaCO₃ should give 5.6 g CaO_  

Check question: `k4-27-theory-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> What is the theoretical mass, in grams, of magnesium oxide from burning 48 g of magnesium completely?

Answer: **80 g**
- Typed **48** (same) → “Oxygen adds to the mass, so the oxide is heavier than the magnesium.”
- Typed **40** (half) → “The ratio is 2 : 2, that is 1 mol of oxide for each 1 mol of magnesium.”

Full working after a second miss:

> 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO: 1 mol of Mg gives 1 mol of MgO.  
> Moles of Mg = 48 ÷ 24 = 2 mol.  
> Mass of MgO = 2 × 40 = 80 g.  

**Card c4k27-3** (draft)

> A yield above 100 per cent means the product is wet or impure.
>
> _dry the product to constant mass before weighing it_  

Check question: `k4-27-over-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> A reaction has a theoretical yield of 20 g. A pupil weighs the product and finds 23 g. By what mass, in grams, must the product be wet or impure?

Answer: **3 g**
- Typed **43** (sum) → “Find the DIFFERENCE between what was weighed and what was possible.”
- Typed **115** (pc) → “That is the percentage yield; the question asks for a mass in grams.”

Full working after a second miss:

> No reaction can give more than its theoretical yield of 20 g.  
> The extra mass must be water or impurity: 23 − 20 = 3 g.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> 10 g of calcium carbonate is heated and 4.4 g of calcium oxide is collected. Find the percentage yield.

1. CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂.
2. Moles of CaCO₃ = 10 ÷ 100 = 0.1 mol.
3. Theoretical mass of CaO = 0.1 × 56 = 5.6 g.
4. Percentage yield = 4.4 ÷ 5.6 × 100.

**Answer:** 78.6 per cent

**Practice** `k4-27-why` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Why can a percentage yield never be more than 100 per cent?

- ◻️ Because some reactant is always left over  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Leftover reactant lowers the yield, it does not raise it._
- ◻️ It can be more than 100 per cent in a good experiment  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A figure above 100 per cent means the product is wet or impure._
- ✅ No more product can be made than the equation allows from the reactants used
- ◻️ Because the balance is not accurate enough  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A more accurate balance would not create extra product._

Full working after a second miss:

> Atoms are conserved: the theoretical yield already counts every atom available.  
> So the answer is No more product can be made than the equation allows from the reactants used.  

> Which of these lowers the percentage yield?

- ◻️ Using a cleaner balance  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A cleaner balance gives a more accurate reading, not a lower yield._
- ✅ Some of the product stays on the filter paper
- ◻️ Writing the equation correctly  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A correct equation gives the right theoretical yield._
- ◻️ Using excess of the other reactant  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Excess of the other reactant does not change what the limiting reagent can give._

Full working after a second miss:

> Any product left behind in the apparatus is not weighed, so the actual yield falls.  
> So the answer is Some of the product stays on the filter paper.

Answer: **60 %**

Full working after a second miss:

> No reaction can give more than its theoretical yield of 50 g.  
> The extra mass must be water or impurity: 51.5 − 50 = 1.5 g.  

## Lesson 28: LLesson 26. Balancing chemical equations using the mole concept. Stoichiometry of chemical reactions

Prerequisites: 25 · Skill: k4-28 (Balance chemical equations using the mole concept)

**Note** (draft)

> A balanced equation has the same number of atoms of each element on both sides, because atoms are neither made nor destroyed. The numbers put in front of the formulae are the coefficients; they give the ratio of moles.
> Rules:
> • Change only the coefficients. Never change a subscript: that would change the substance.
> • Balance the element that appears in fewest places first, and leave hydrogen and oxygen to the end.
> • Count again at the end, element by element.
> Example: balance Fe2O3 + CO → Fe + CO2.
> Iron: 2 on the left, so put 2 in front of Fe. Oxygen: 3 + 1 = 4 on the left against 2 on the right; trying 3CO and 3CO2 gives 3 + 3 = 6 on each side. The balanced equation is Fe2O3 + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO2.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to balance an equation by changing only the coefficients, and read the mole ratio from it.
> Builds on: Form Three balancing simple equations and Lesson 25 (using the ratio).
> Leads to: Lesson 29 (adding state symbols) and every calculation that starts from an equation.

**Learn: Why and how to balance** (draft)

> In a chemical reaction atoms are neither made nor destroyed; they are only rearranged. So an equation must have the same number of atoms of each element on both sides.
> The numbers written IN FRONT of the formulae are the coefficients. They may be changed freely. The small numbers INSIDE a formula are subscripts; they must never be changed, because they say what the substance is. Changing H₂O to H₂O₂ turns water into hydrogen peroxide.
> A useful order:
> 1. balance the element that appears in the fewest places;
> 2. leave hydrogen and oxygen until last;
> 3. count every element again at the end.

**Learn: A worked example and the mole ratio** (draft)

> Balance Fe₂O₃ + CO → Fe + CO₂.
> • Iron: 2 on the left, so write 2Fe.
> • Oxygen: 3 + 1 = 4 on the left, 2 on the right. Try 3CO and 3CO₂: 3 + 3 = 6 on the left and 6 on the right.
> • Carbon: 3 and 3. Balanced: Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂.
> Once balanced, the coefficients give the MOLE RATIO, which is what every calculation uses: 1 mol of Fe₂O₃ needs 3 mol of CO and gives 2 mol of iron.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **coefficient**: the number written in front of a formula
- **subscript**: the small number inside a formula, which must not be changed
- **conservation of matter**: atoms are neither made nor destroyed in a reaction
- **mole ratio**: the ratio given by the coefficients

- 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO
- CH₄ + 2O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O

Common mistake: “balance H₂ + O₂ → H₂O by writing H₂O₂” → “write 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O”. Changing the subscript changes the substance. Only coefficients may be changed.

**Card c4k28-1** (draft)

> Balance by changing coefficients only; never change a subscript.
>
> _H₂O must stay H₂O, not become H₂O₂_  

Check question: `k4-28-rules` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> In Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂, how many moles of carbon monoxide react with 1 mol of iron(III) oxide?

- ◻️ 1 mol  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The coefficient of CO is 3, not 1._
- ◻️ 6 mol  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: 6 is twice the coefficient of the carbon monoxide._
- ✅ 3 mol
- ◻️ 2 mol  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: 2 is the coefficient of the iron._

Full working after a second miss:

> Read the coefficient in front of CO: it is 3.  
> So the answer is 3 mol.  

**Card c4k28-2** (draft)

> The coefficients give the ratio of moles.
>
> _2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O is 2 : 1 : 2 by moles_  

Check question: `k4-28-coef` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> In the equation Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂, what is the coefficient of CO?

Answer: **3**
- Typed **1** (one) → “A number is written in front of CO in this equation: read it again.”
- Typed **2** (sub) → “Read the number in FRONT of the formula, not a subscript inside it.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Read the number written in front of CO.  
> It is 3. (A formula with no number in front has a coefficient of 1.)  

**Card c4k28-3** (draft)

> Count every element again at the end.
>
> _Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂: O is 6 on each side_  

Check question: `k4-28-count-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> Which equation is correctly balanced?

- ◻️ N₂ + 2H₂ → 2NH₃  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Count: N 2 and 2; H 6 and 6._
- ✅ N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃
- ◻️ N₂ + 3H₂ → NH₃  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Count: N 2 and 2; H 6 and 6._
- ◻️ N₂ + H₂ → NH₃  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Count: N 2 and 2; H 6 and 6._

Full working after a second miss:

> Count: N 2 and 2; H 6 and 6.  
> So the answer is: N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Balance Fe₂O₃ + CO → Fe + CO₂.

1. Iron appears in fewest places: 2 on the left, so write 2Fe on the right.
2. Carbon: 1 on each side so far; oxygen: 3 + 1 = 4 on the left and 2 on the right.
3. Try 3CO and 3CO₂: oxygen becomes 3 + 3 = 6 on the left and 6 on the right.
4. Count again: Fe 2 and 2; C 3 and 3; O 6 and 6.

**Answer:** Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂

**Practice** `k4-28-rules` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Why must a chemical equation be balanced?

- ◻️ So that the masses of the reactants are equal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The two reactants may have very different masses; it is the atoms that must match._
- ◻️ To make the equation look tidy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Balancing is not about appearance; it states a law of nature._
- ◻️ So that there is the same number of molecules on both sides  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The number of MOLECULES often changes; it is the atoms that are conserved._
- ✅ Atoms are neither made nor destroyed, so each element must have the same number of atoms on both sides

Full working after a second miss:

> Balancing states the conservation of matter: the same atoms are simply rearranged.  
> So the answer is Atoms are neither made nor destroyed, so each element must have the same number of atoms on both sides.  

> What may be changed when balancing an equation?

- ◻️ Nothing at all  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The coefficients are exactly what is adjusted._
- ✅ Only the coefficients, the numbers in front of the formulae
- ◻️ The subscripts inside the formulae  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Changing a subscript changes the substance: H₂O is water, but H₂O₂ is not._
- ◻️ Both the subscripts and the coefficients  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Subscripts must never be changed; they belong to the formula._

Full working after a second miss:

> A coefficient says how many units react; a subscript says what the substance IS.  
> So the answer is Only the coefficients, the numbers in front of the formulae.  

**Practice** `k4-28-coef` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

Answer: **2**
- Typed **1** (one) → “A number is written in front of H₂O in this equation: read it again.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Read the number written in front of H₂O.  
> It is 2. (A formula with no number in front has a coefficient of 1.)  

> In the equation Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂, what is the coefficient of CO?

Answer: **3**
- Typed **1** (one) → “A number is written in front of CO in this equation: read it again.”
- Typed **2** (sub) → “Read the number in FRONT of the formula, not a subscript inside it.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Read the number written in front of CO.  
> It is 3. (A formula with no number in front has a coefficient of 1.)

> Which equation is correctly balanced?

- ✅ 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO

Full working after a second miss:

**Practice** `k4-28-moles` · number, level 2 · computed by rule

> In N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃, how many moles of hydrogen are needed for 2.5 mol of nitrogen?

Answer: **7.5 mol**
- Typed **5** (two) → “2 is the coefficient of the ammonia, not of the hydrogen.”
- Typed **2.5** (same) → “Each mole of nitrogen needs 3 mol of hydrogen.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The ratio of N₂ to H₂ is 1 : 3.  
> 2.5 × 3 = 7.5 mol.  

> In N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃, how many moles of hydrogen are needed for 0.5 mol of nitrogen?

Answer: **1.5 mol**
- Typed **1** (two) → “2 is the coefficient of the ammonia, not of the hydrogen.”
- Typed **0.5** (same) → “Each mole of nitrogen needs 3 mol of hydrogen.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The ratio of N₂ to H₂ is 1 : 3.  
> 0.5 × 3 = 1.5 mol.  

**Practice** `k4-28-mass` · number, level 3 · computed by rule

> In 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO, what mass of oxygen, in grams, reacts with 4.8 g of magnesium?

Answer: **3.2 g**
- Typed **6.4** (noratio) → “The ratio is 2 mol of Mg to 1 mol of O₂: halve it.”
- Typed **1.6** (atom) → “Oxygen gas is O₂, of molar mass 32.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Moles of Mg = 4.8 ÷ 24 = 0.2 mol.  
> The ratio of Mg to O₂ is 2 : 1, so moles of O₂ = 0.1 mol.  
> Mass = 0.1 × 32 = 3.2 g.  

> In 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO, what mass of oxygen, in grams, reacts with 12 g of magnesium?

Answer: **8 g**
- Typed **16** (noratio) → “The ratio is 2 mol of Mg to 1 mol of O₂: halve it.”
- Typed **4** (atom) → “Oxygen gas is O₂, of molar mass 32.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Moles of Mg = 12 ÷ 24 = 0.5 mol.  
> The ratio of Mg to O₂ is 2 : 1, so moles of O₂ = 0.25 mol.  
> Mass = 0.25 × 32 = 8 g.  

## Lesson 29: Write chemical equations including state symbols

Prerequisites: 28 · Skill: k4-29 (Write chemical equations including state symbols)

**Note** (draft)

> State symbols are written after each formula to show the physical state:
> • (s) solid  • (l) pure liquid  • (g) gas  • (aq) dissolved in water (aqueous)
> Examples:
> Zn(s) + H2SO4(aq) → ZnSO4(aq) + H2(g)
> NaOH(aq) + HCl(aq) → NaCl(aq) + H2O(l)
> Pb(NO3)2(aq) + 2KI(aq) → PbI2(s) + 2KNO3(aq)
> They are useful because they tell you what would be seen: an (s) formed from (aq) solutions is a precipitate, a (g) means bubbles, and water formed in a neutralisation is (l), not (aq).
> A full equation is balanced first, then the state symbols are added.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to add state symbols to a balanced equation and use them to say what would be seen.
> Builds on: Lesson 28 (balancing) and Form Three states of matter.
> Leads to: writing the full equations required in examinations, and ionic equations in Form Five.

**Learn: The four symbols** (draft)

> A state symbol is written just after each formula:
> • (s) solid  • (l) pure liquid  • (g) gas  • (aq) aqueous, that is dissolved in water.
> Take care with (l) and (aq). Water made in a neutralisation is H₂O(l), because it is water itself. Salt dissolved in water is NaCl(aq), because it is dissolved IN water.
> Write the equation and balance it first; the state symbols are added last, and never change the balancing.

**Learn: Reading what would be seen** (draft)

> State symbols turn an equation into a description of the experiment.
> • Zn(s) + H₂SO₄(aq) → ZnSO₄(aq) + H₂(g): the solid disappears and bubbles are given off.
> • Pb(NO₃)₂(aq) + 2KI(aq) → PbI₂(s) + 2KNO₃(aq): two clear solutions give a yellow precipitate.
> • NaOH(aq) + HCl(aq) → NaCl(aq) + H₂O(l): nothing is seen at all, though the mixture warms up.
> So an (s) among (aq) substances means a precipitate, and a (g) means bubbles.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **state symbol**: (s), (l), (g) or (aq), showing the physical state
- **aqueous**: dissolved in water
- **precipitate**: an insoluble solid formed when two solutions are mixed
- **neutralisation**: the reaction of an acid with a base to give a salt and water

- Zn(s) + H₂SO₄(aq) → ZnSO₄(aq) + H₂(g)
- NaOH(aq) + HCl(aq) → NaCl(aq) + H₂O(l)

Common mistake: “the water made in a neutralisation is written H₂O(aq)” → “it is H₂O(l)”. (aq) means dissolved in water; water cannot be dissolved in itself.

**Card c4k29-1** (draft)

> (s) solid, (l) pure liquid, (g) gas, (aq) dissolved in water.
>
> _NaCl(aq) is salt solution; H₂O(l) is water itself_  

Check question: `k4-29-which` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What does the state symbol (aq) mean?

- ◻️ A pure liquid  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A pure liquid is shown by (l), such as water itself._
- ◻️ A solid  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A solid is shown by (s)._
- ◻️ A gas  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A gas is shown by (g)._
- ✅ Dissolved in water: an aqueous solution

Full working after a second miss:

> (aq) shows that the substance is dissolved in water, as in NaCl(aq).  
> So the answer is Dissolved in water: an aqueous solution.  

**Card c4k29-2** (draft)

> An (s) made from (aq) solutions is a precipitate.
>
> _PbI₂(s) from two solutions: a yellow precipitate_

> What would be seen in the reaction Pb(NO₃)₂(aq) + 2KI(aq) → PbI₂(s) + 2KNO₃(aq)?

- ◻️ Bubbles of gas are given off  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An (s) formed from (aq) solutions is a precipitate: yellow lead(II) iodide._
- ◻️ Nothing at all  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An (s) formed from (aq) solutions is a precipitate: yellow lead(II) iodide._
- ✅ A precipitate forms from two clear solutions
- ◻️ The mixture boils  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An (s) formed from (aq) solutions is a precipitate: yellow lead(II) iodide._

Full working after a second miss:

> An (s) formed from (aq) solutions is a precipitate: yellow lead(II) iodide.  
> So the answer is: A precipitate forms from two clear solutions.  

**Card c4k29-3** (draft)

> Balance the equation first, then add the state symbols.
>
> _CaCO₃(s) + 2HCl(aq) → CaCl₂(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g)_

> Which state symbol belongs to the carbon dioxide given off by a carbonate in an equation at room temperature?

- ◻️ (l)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Carbon dioxide bubbles away as a gas._
- ◻️ (aq)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Carbon dioxide bubbles away as a gas._
- ◻️ (s)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Carbon dioxide bubbles away as a gas._

Full working after a second miss:

> Carbon dioxide bubbles away as a gas.  
> So the answer is: (g).  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write the equation, with state symbols, for adding dilute hydrochloric acid to solid calcium carbonate.

1. The reactants are solid calcium carbonate and hydrochloric acid solution.
2. A carbonate with an acid gives a salt, water and carbon dioxide.
3. Balance first: CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂.
4. Then add the states: the carbonate is (s), the acid and the salt are (aq), the water is (l) and the carbon dioxide is (g).

**Answer:** CaCO₃(s) + 2HCl(aq) → CaCl₂(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g)

> (aq) shows that the substance is dissolved in water, as in NaCl(aq).

> Why are state symbols useful in an equation?

- ◻️ They make the equation balance  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Balancing is done with coefficients; state symbols do not affect it._
- ✅ They show what would be seen: a precipitate, a gas given off, or a clear solution
- ◻️ They give the mass of each substance  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Masses come from the moles and the molar masses._
- ◻️ They show how fast the reaction goes  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Speed is not shown by an equation at all._

Full working after a second miss:

> A (s) among (aq) substances means a precipitate; a (g) means bubbles.  
> So the answer is They show what would be seen: a precipitate, a gas given off, or a clear solution.
